Monoidal Pull-Push II: Local Systems
Angus Rush

Abstract




1
Introduction and conventions

1.1
Introduction

In topology, one often studies spaces via algebraic invariants associated to them. One such invariant of particular interest is the
n
th homology
of a space
X
, denoted
H_{n}(X)
. Roughly speaking,
H_{n}(X)
is the abelian group consisting of formal sums of certain maps from the standard
n
-simplex
\Delta^{n}
into
X
, considered modulo some equivalence relation.

It turns out to be fruitful to generalize
n
th homology. Formal sums are nothing else but
\mathbb{Z}
-linear combinations, and one can ask what happens when
\mathbb{Z}
is replaced by some other commutative ring
R
. The associated invariant is then known as
n
th homology with coefficients in
R
. One can even allow the ring
R
to vary along
X
, or allow
R
to be not only a ring, but an object of some appropriate category
\mathcal{C}
. The structure which keeps track of these changing coefficients is called a
\mathcal{C}
-
local system
.

Classically speaking, when we say
space,
we really mean
topological space,
and
\mathcal{C}
-local systems are modelled by locally-constant sheaves with values in
\mathcal{C}
. In modern homotopy theory, one often takes
space
to mean
\infty
-groupoid;
thought of in this way, a
\mathcal{C}
-local system on a space
X
is nothing more than a functor
X\to\mathcal{C}
. This is the point of view that we will take.

With this point of view in mind, let
\mathcal{C}
be a cocomplete
\infty
-category. For any space
X
, denote by
\mathbf{LS}(\mathcal{C})_{X}
the
\infty
-category of
\mathcal{C}
-local systems on
X
; that is,
\mathbf{LS}(\mathcal{C})_{X}\simeq\mathrm{Fun}(X,\mathcal{C})
. Recall that for any morphism
f\colon X\to Y
of spaces, there are several associated functors between
\mathbf{LS}(\mathcal{C})_{X}
and
\mathbf{LS}(\mathcal{C})_{Y}
. In particular:

•

The
pullback functor

f^{*}\colon\mathbf{LS}(\mathcal{C})_{Y}\to\mathbf{LS}(\mathcal{C})_{X}
pulls back local systems on
Y
to local systems on
X
, sending a local system
\mathcal{F}\colon Y\to\mathcal{C}
to the local system
f^{*}\mathcal{F}
given by the composition

The
pushforward functor

f_{!}\colon\mathbf{LS}(\mathcal{C})_{X}\to\mathbf{LS}(\mathcal{C})_{Y}
pushes forward local systems via left Kan extension, sending a local system
\mathcal{G}\colon X\to\mathcal{C}
to the left Kan extension
f_{!}\mathcal{G}
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{!}G}

Note that pulling back is contravariantly functorial, and pushing forward is covariantly functorial. One can combine these functorialities. Given the data of a diagram of spaces of the form

The data of
Diagram
1
is known as a
span
of spaces. It is natural to ask whether this construction can be extended to a functor
\mathrm{Span}(\mathcal{S})\to\mathcal{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}
, where
\mathrm{Span}(\mathcal{S})
is an
\infty
-category whose objects are spaces, and whose morphisms are spans of spaces.

This is the second part of a two-part paper. In the first part, we provided a simplified proof of a theorem of Barwick
[
2
, Thm. 12.2]
, providing several sufficient conditions for a functor of quasicategories
p\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
to yield a cocartesian fibration between
\infty
-categories of spans
\mathrm{Span}(p)\colon\mathrm{Span}(\mathcal{C})\to\mathrm{Span}(\mathcal{D})
, and hence a functor
\hat{r}\colon\mathrm{Span}(\mathcal{D})\to\mathcal{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}
via the Grothendieck construction. One such sufficient condition is that
p
be a so-called
Beck–Chevalley fibration
(
Definition
4.0.1
). Roughly speaking, a Beck–Chevalley fibration is a bicartesian fibration satisfying a straightened version of the Beck–Chevalley condition. More information about Beck–Chevalley fibrations is given in the introduction to
Section
4
of this work; a more in-depth introduction is given in
[
7
, Sec. 4.1]
. It is an easy consequence of the theory of Beck–Chevalley fibrations that there exists a functor
\hat{r}\colon\mathrm{Span}(\mathcal{S})\to\mathcal{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}
, which sends a space
X
to the category
\mathbf{LS}(\mathcal{C})_{X}
of local systems on
X
, and a morphism in
\mathrm{Span}(\mathcal{S})
represented by a span

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

to the functor
f_{!}\circ g^{*}\colon\mathbf{LS}(\mathcal{C})_{X}\to\mathbf{LS}(\mathcal{C})_{X^{\prime}}
. This is the main result which we will prove in
Section
4
.

In this paper, we introduce a generalization of Beck–Chevalley fibrations, which we call
monoidal Beck–Chevalley fibrations.
Roughly speaking, a monoidal Beck–Chevalley fibration
\tilde{p}\colon\mathcal{C}_{\otimes}\to\mathcal{D}_{\boxtimes}
is an enhancement of an ordinary Beck–Chevalley fibration
p\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
, which takes into account monoidal structures on
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{D}
, and provides a lax monoidal structure structure on the functor
\mathrm{Span}(\mathcal{D})\to\mathcal{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}
.

We use the theory of monoidal Beck–Chevalley fibrations to show that our functor
\hat{r}\colon\mathrm{Span}(\mathcal{S})\to\mathcal{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}
constructed in
Section
4
admits a lax monoidal structure with respect to a monoidal structure on
\mathrm{Span}(\mathcal{S})
induced by the cartesian structure on
\mathcal{S}
, and the cartesian structure on
\mathcal{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}
.

1.2
Relation to previous work

That pull-push of local systems can be written as a symmetric monoidal functor out of a category of spans is far from a new idea. Our approach differs from previous ones, however, in that our aim is to provide a simple, explicit construction of monoidal pull-push of local systems, without any pretensions of working in complete generality when it does not, in our view, lead to greater conceptual clarity.

There have been (to the knowledge of the author) two main works with results similar to those in this paper.

•

In
[
3
]
, similar results to those in
Subsection
5.2
, about monoidal Beck-Chevalley fibrations, are stated in a rather different situation. The application to local systems is not considered. We have treated these results in less generality.

•

In
[
5
]
, results about extending a functor out of a category of spans are proved, but more generally, more abstractly, and for the most part model-independently; there, spans come in
(\infty,2)
-categories, modelled when necessary by complete 2-fold Segal spaces. Our approach is more explicit, based on hands-on combinatorial computations done in a quasicategorical twisted arrow category. We believe that the explicitness of our model will be useful in our future work.

1.3
Outline

In
Section
2
, we generalize the universal property satisfied by Kan extensions of
1
-categories to
\infty
-categorical Kan extensions, and show how this universal property can be used to solve lifting problems in
\mathbbm{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}
.

Our proof that left Kan extensions enjoy this lifting property uses that left Kan extension is left adjoint to restriction; this can be used even if not all left Kan extensions along a functor exist. To formalize this, we introduce in
Subsection
2.3
the notion of a
partial adjunction,
and use this to show that data on one side of an adjunction of
\infty
-categories can be used to fill adjunct data on the other side. Using this, we show in
Subsection
2.4
that this generalized lifting property really is satisfied.

In
Section
3
, we apply our results about left Kan extensions to show, via an explicit computation in an appropriate model (an
\infty
-categorical twisted arrow category), that left Kan extensions of local systems are
\infty
-functorial, i.e. extends to a functor
\mathcal{S}\to\mathcal{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}
. We then use this to show that pull-push functoriality extends to a functor
\mathrm{Span}(\mathcal{S})\to\mathcal{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}
. In
Section
5
, we show that this construction can be endowed with a lax monoidal structure.

1.4
General conventions and set-theoretic size issues

We do our level best to adhere to the following terminological and typographical practices regarding various types of categories.

•

When we say ‘
\infty
-groupoid,’ we will mean an
(\infty,0)
-category, here always modelled by a Kan complex. We will in general denote
\infty
-groupoids by capital roman letters coming from the end of the alphabet:
X
,
Y
, …

•

When we say ‘
\infty
-category,’ we will mean an
(\infty,1)
-category, usually modelled by a quasicategory. We will strive to denote
\infty
-categories by calligraphic letters typeset using the
eucal
package:
\mathcal{C}
,
\mathcal{D}
, …

•

When we say ‘
\infty
-bicategory,’ we will mean an
(\infty,2)
-category in the sense of
[
9
]
, modelled by a fibrant scaled or marked-scaled
1
1

1

The theory of marked-scaled simplicial sets is given in
[
1
]
, and summarized in
Subsection
1.5
.
simplicial set; which one we mean should be clear from context. We will denote
(\infty,2)
-categories by blackboard-bold letters typeset with the
mathbbol
package:
\mathbbm{C}
,
\mathbbm{D}
, …

We follow Lurie in our definitions of categories of
\infty
-categories (except for a few size-related transgressions explained below). We also adhere to the above typographical conventions in doing so. Thus:

•

The
(\infty,1)
-category of spaces is
\mathcal{S}
. We model this as a quasicategory, constructed (as in
[
10
]
) as the homotopy-coherent nerve (as described in
[
10
, Sec. 1.1.5]
, there called the simplicial nerve) of
\mathbf{Kan}
, the Kan-enriched category of Kan complexes.

•

The
(\infty,1)
-category of
(\infty,1)
-categories is
\mathcal{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}
. We model this as a quasicategory, defined to be the homotopy-coherent nerve of
\mathbf{QCat}
, the Kan-enriched category of quasicategories.

•

The
(\infty,2)
-category of
(\infty,1)
-categories is
\mathbbm{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}
. We model this as an
\infty
-bicategory, defined to be the scaled nerve (as described in
[
9
, Sec. 3.1]
) of the
\mathcal{S}\mathrm{et}_{\Delta}^{+}
-enriched category of quasicategories.

•

The
(\infty,1)
-category of
(\infty,2)
-categories is
\mathcal{C}\mathrm{at}_{(\infty,2)}
. This is used only briefly, and will be modelled as the simplicial localization of
(\mathcal{S}\mathrm{et}_{\Delta}^{\mathbf{MS}})^{f}
, the full subcategory of marked-scaled simplicial sets on fibrant objects, at the class of bicategorical equivalences.

For the most part, our conventions regarding set-theoretic size issues are standard. However, we will at several points point need to consider an
\infty
-category whose objects are large
\infty
-categories. The rigorous solution would be to introduce a series of nested Grothendieck universe, keep track of which one we are currently in, and carry this around as extra notation. However, there are no arguments in this paper which hinge on any set-theoretic size-issues, and the author feels that clarity is lost, rather than gained, by introducing this extraneous notation. Therefore, we will use the same notation for the large
\infty
-category of small
\infty
-categories and the ‘huge’
\infty
-category of large
\infty
-categories.

Suppose
K
is a simplicial set and
\mathcal{C}
is an
\infty
-category. By
\mathrm{Fun}(K,\mathcal{C})
, we mean the
\infty
-category of maps
K\to\mathcal{C}
. By
\mathrm{Map}(K,\mathcal{C})
, we mean the
\infty
-groupoid of such maps; that is,

\mathrm{Map}(K,\mathcal{C})=\mathrm{Fun}(K,\mathcal{C})^{\simeq},

where
(-)^{\simeq}\colon\mathcal{S}\mathrm{et}_{\Delta}\to\mathbf{Kan}
denotes the
core
functor, i.e. the functor associating to a simplicial set
K
the largest Kan complex contained in it.

1.5
The marked-scaled model structure

We will need two different models for
(\infty,2)
-categories: Lurie’s theory of scaled simplicial sets, as laid out in
[
9
]
; and Abellán–Stern’s theory of marked-scaled simplicial sets, as explained in
[
1
]
. We will assume a knowledge of scaled simplicial sets. We give a basic outline of the portions of the theory of marked-scaled simplicial sets which we will need.

A marked-scaled simplicial set is a triple
(X,E_{X},T_{X})
, where
E_{X}\subseteq X_{1}
is a collection of edges of
X
containing all degenerate edges, and
T_{X}\subseteq X_{2}
is a collection of triangles of
X
containing all degenerate triangles. We will use the following notation.

•

We will denote the category of marked-scaled simplicial sets by
\mathcal{S}\mathrm{et}_{\Delta}^{\mathbf{MS}}
.

•

To save on notation, we will sometimes denote the marked-scaled simplicial set
(X,E_{X},T_{X})
by
X^{E_{X}}_{T_{X}}
, particularly in the case that
E_{X}
or
T_{X}
are
\sharp
or
\flat
, the maximum (resp. minimum) markings and scalings. For example, the bimarked simplicial set
X^{\sharp}_{\flat}
has all 1-simplices marked and only degenerate 2-simplices scaled.

•

Let
(\Delta^{n},E,T)
be a marked-scaled
n
-simplex. For any simplicial subset
S\subseteq\Delta^{n}
, we will denote by
(S,E,T)
the simplicial subset
S
together with the inherited marking and scaling.

There is a set of marked-scaled anodyne morphisms, which have the left lifting property with respect to marked-scaled fibrations. We will not need to use the full power of the marked-scaled anodyne morphisms, so we content ourselves with an incomplete description.

Definition 1.5.1
.

The set of ms-anodyne morphisms is a saturated set of morphisms between marked-scaled simplicial sets containing the following classes of morphisms:

(A1)

Inner horn inclusions

(\Lambda^{n}_{i},\flat,\{\Delta^{\{i-1,i,i+1\}}\})\to(\Delta^{n},\flat,\{\Delta^{\{i-1,i,i+1\}}\}),

for
n\geq 2
and
0<i<n
.

(A2)

Outer horn inclusions

(\Lambda^{n}_{n},\{\Delta^{\{n-1,n\}}\},\{\Delta^{\{0,n-1,n\}}\}),

for
n\geq 1
.

Example 1.5.2
.

The marked-scaled anodyne morphisms encapsulate both left- and marked-anodyne morphisms.

•

For any right anodyne morphism
A\hookrightarrow B
, the morphism
A^{\sharp}_{\sharp}\hookrightarrow B^{\sharp}_{\sharp}
is marked-scaled anodyne.

•

For any marked anodyne morphism
(A,\mathcal{E})\to(B,\mathcal{F})
, the morphism
A^{\mathcal{E}}_{\sharp}\to B^{\mathcal{F}}_{\sharp}
is marked-scaled anodyne.

Theorem 1.5.3
.

There is a model structure on the category
\mathcal{S}\mathrm{et}_{\Delta}^{\mathbf{MS}}
, whose trivial cofibrations are contain the set of marked-scaled anodyne maps, and whose fibrant objects are
\infty
-bicategories with the equivalences marked and thin simplices scaled.

Theorem 1.5.4
.

There is a Quillen equivalence

(-)_{\flat}:\mathcal{S}\mathrm{et}_{\Delta}^{\mathrm{sc}}\longleftrightarrow\mathcal{S}\mathrm{et}_{\Delta}^{\mathbf{MS}}:G,

where
(-)^{\flat}
endows any scaled simplicial set with the flat marking, and
G
forgets the marking.

1.6
Selected results from Monoidal Pull-Push I

We will at several points make use of the results of the first part of this paper, Monoidal Pull-Push I. We reproduce here our main theorems there, both of which will be instrumental in this paper. These give sufficient conditions on a functor
p\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
so that the induced functor
\mathrm{Span}(\mathcal{C})\to\mathrm{Span}(\mathcal{D})
is a cocartesian fibration. Here, the subcategories
\mathcal{C}_{\dagger}
and
\mathcal{C}^{\dagger}
pick out distinguished classes of morphisms to which the legs of the spans of
\mathrm{Span}(\mathcal{C})
are allowed to belong; the ‘forwards-facing’ legs of
\mathrm{Span}(\mathcal{C})
are restricted to come from the subcategory
\mathcal{C}_{\dagger}
, and the ‘forward-facing’ legs from
\mathcal{C}^{\dagger}
; and similarly for
\mathcal{D}_{\dagger}
and
\mathcal{D}^{\dagger}
.

Theorem 1.6.1
.

Let
p\colon(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}_{\dagger},\mathcal{C}^{\dagger})\to(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{D}_{\dagger},\mathcal{D}^{\dagger})
be a functor between adequate triples such that
p\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
is an inner fibration which satisfies the following conditions.

1.

Each morphism
g\in\mathcal{D}_{\dagger}
admits a lift to a morphism in
\mathcal{C}_{\dagger}
(given a lift of the source) which is both
p
-cocartesian and
p_{\dagger}
-cocartesian.

2.

in
\mathcal{C}
where

belongs to
\mathcal{C}^{\dagger}
, and

and

belong to
\mathcal{C}_{\dagger}
. Suppose that
f
is
p
-cocartesian. Then

is

-cocartesian if and only if
\sigma
is an ambigressive pullback square (and in particular
g\in\mathcal{C}^{\dagger}
).

Then spans of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

are cocartesian, where
g
is
p^{\dagger}
-cartesian and
f
is
p
-cocartesian.

Theorem 1.6.2
.

Let
p\colon(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}_{\dagger},\mathcal{C}^{\dagger})\to(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{D}_{\dagger},\mathcal{D}^{\dagger})
be a functor between adequate triples such that
p\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
is an inner fibration which satisfies the following conditions.

1.

The subcategory
\mathcal{C}^{\dagger}\subseteq\mathcal{C}
consists of all
p
-cartesian morphisms in
\mathcal{C}
; that is, an
n
-simplex in
\mathcal{C}
belongs to
\mathcal{C}^{\dagger}
if and only if each
1
-simplex it contains is
p
-cartesian.

2.

The map
p^{\dagger}\colon\mathcal{C}^{\dagger}\to\mathcal{D}^{\dagger}
is a cartesian fibration.

3.

Consider a square

\sigma=\quad\hbox to77.81pt{\vbox to51.34pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 38.90504pt\lower-24.17558pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-38.90504pt}{-18.57143pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}}

2
Horn filling via left Kan extensions

Kan extensions of
\infty
-categories are usually defined pointwise via the colimit formula
[
10
]

[
4
]
. In this section, we will show that such pointwise
\infty
-Kan extensions enjoy a horn filling property in
\mathbbm{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}
which generalizes the universal property for Kan extensions of functors between
1
-categories.

We will explain the precise meaning of the below definition in
Subsection
2.1
.

.

if the natural transformation
\eta
exhibits

as the left Kan extension of

along
f
.

The goal of this section is to prove the following.

Theorem 2.0.2
.

Let
\tau\colon\Delta^{2}_{\flat}\to\mathbbm{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}
be a left Kan 2-simplex. Then for each
n\geq 2
, every solid lifting problem of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta^{\{0,1,n+1\}}_{\flat}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\Lambda^{n+1}_{0})_{\flat}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbbm{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta^{n+1}_{\flat}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau}

(3)

admits a dashed solution.

Note 2.0.3
.

One can even show that if the terminal vertex in
\tau
is mapped to an
\infty
-category
\mathcal{C}
with sufficient colimits (i.e. all colimits in
\mathcal{C}
indexed by the homotopy fibers of
f
should exist), then
\tau
is left kan
if and only if
it enjoys these lifting properties. That is, these lifting properties generalize the universal property for Kan extensions of ordinary categories. However, we will not make use of this result.

Let us examine the lifting problems of
Equation
3
in the case
n=2
. In this case we have the data of categories and functors

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{!}F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

together with natural transformations
\eta\colon F\Rightarrow f_{!}F\circ f
,
\beta\colon h\Rightarrow g\circ f
, and
\delta\colon F\Rightarrow G\circ h
, making up the sides of
\Lambda^{3}_{0}
. We need to produce a natural transformation
\alpha\colon f_{!}F\Rightarrow G\circ g
and a filling of the full 3-simplex, which is the data of a homomotopy-commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{!}F\circ f}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\circ h}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\circ g\circ f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\beta}

in
\mathrm{Fun}(X,\mathcal{C})
.
2
2

2

In particular, if we take
h=f
,
g=\mathrm{id}_{Y}
, and
\beta
to be the identity
f\Rightarrow f
, we recover the classical universal property satisfied by left Kan extension.

The lifting problems which we have to solve in
Equation
3
for
n>2
amount to replacing
\Delta^{1}\times\Delta^{1}
by
(\Delta^{1})^{n}
, etc; the basic process remains unchanged, but the combinatorics involved in filling the necessary cubes becomes more involved. In
Subsection
2.2
we explain the combinatorics of filling cubes relative to their boundaries. In
Subsection
2.3
, we give a formalization the concept of adjunct data, and provide a means of for filling partial data to total adjunct data. In
Subsection
2.4
, we show that we can always solve the lifting problems of
Equation
3
.

2.1
Basic facts about Kan extensions

In this section, we recall a few basic facts about Kan extensions. These are mostly results found in
[
11
]
which we will need. Since Kan extensions are defined by the colimit formula, we will start by defining colimits. Clasically, colimits in
\infty
-categories are defined using colimit cones.

Definition 2.1.1
.

Let
F\colon K\to\mathcal{C}
be a diagram, where
\mathcal{C}
is an
\infty
-category. A cocone
\tilde{F}\colon K^{\triangleright}\to\mathcal{C}
is a
colimit cone
if it is an initial object in the
\infty
-category
\mathcal{C}_{F/}
.

It is sometimes more convenient to define colimits via natural transformations to a constant functor.

Definition 2.1.2
.

Let
f\colon K\to\mathcal{C}
be a map between simplicial sets, where
\mathcal{C}
is an
\infty
-category. Let
c\in\mathcal{C}
be an object, and denote by
\underline{c}
the constant functor
K\to\mathcal{C}
with value
c
. A natural transformation
f\Rightarrow\underline{c}

exhibits
c
as the colimit of
f
if it is an initial object in the
\infty
-category
\mathcal{C}^{F/}
.

Fortunately, these definitions are compatible: an object
K^{\triangleright}\to\mathcal{C}
in
\mathcal{C}_{/F}
is a colimit cone with cone tip
c
if and only if the composite map

K\times\Delta^{1}\to K\times\Delta^{1}\amalg_{K\times\{1\}}\Delta^{0}\to K\star\Delta^{0}\to\mathcal{C}

(4)

exhibits
c
as a colimit of
F
. This follows from the fact that for any
\infty
-category
\mathcal{C}
and any functor
F\colon K\to\mathcal{C}
, the comparison map
\mathcal{C}_{/F}\to\mathcal{C}^{/F}
of
[
10
, Prop. 4.2.1.5]
is a categorical equivalence.

Note 2.1.3
.

It follows immediately from this description that colimit cones are homotopically invariant: if a natural transformation
\eta
exhibits some object as a colimit of some diagram, than any natural transformation
\eta^{\prime}
which is homotopic to
\eta
will do just as well.

We take a moment to record a definition, which we will need later.

Definition 2.1.4
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be an
\infty
-category, and let
F\colon\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}
be a functor. We say that
F
preserves colimits in each slot
if and only if the following condition is satisfed:

•

Let
K
and
K^{\prime}
be simplicial sets; let
G\colon K\to\mathcal{C}
and
G^{\prime}\colon K^{\prime}\to\mathcal{C}
be functors; and suppose
\eta\colon G\Rightarrow\underline{c}
exhibits
c
as the colimit of
G
, and
\eta^{\prime}\colon G^{\prime}\Rightarrow\underline{c^{\prime}}
exhibits
c^{\prime}
as the colimit of
G^{\prime}
. Then
F(\eta\times\eta^{\prime})\colon F\circ(G\times G^{\prime})\Rightarrow\underline{F(c,c^{\prime})}
exhibits
F(c,c^{\prime})
as the colimit of
F\circ(G\times G^{\prime})
.

Notation 2.1.5
.

Let
f\colon X\to Y
be a map of simplicial sets, and
y\in Y
an object. We will use the following notation.

•

Denote by
X_{/y}
the fiber product
X\times_{Y}Y_{/y}
.

•

Denote by
\pi\colon X_{/y}\to X
the projection map.

•

Denote by
\alpha\colon f\circ\pi\Rightarrow\underline{y}
the natural transformation
X_{/y}\times\Delta^{1}\to X
coming from the comparison map
X_{/y}\to X^{/y}
.

Definition 2.1.6

(
[
11
, Variant 7.3.1.5]
)
.

Let
X
,
Y
, and
\mathcal{C}
be
\infty
-categories,
f\colon X\to Y
,
F\colon X\to\mathcal{C}
and
G\colon Y\to\mathcal{C}
functors, and
\eta\colon F\Rightarrow G\circ f
a natural transformation.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}

We say that
\eta

exhibits
G
as the left Kan extension of
F
along
f
if for each object
y\in Y
, the natural transformation
F\circ\pi\Rightarrow\underline{G(y)}
furnished by the pasting diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{/y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{y\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}

exhibits
G(y)
as the colimit of the functor
F\circ\pi
. Here,
\underline{G(y)}
is the ‘counterclockwise’ path from
X_{/y}
to
\mathcal{C}
.

The following guarantees the existence of local left Kan extensions.

Theorem 2.1.7

(
[
11
, Proposition 7.3.5.1]
)
.

Suppose that
f\colon X\to Y
is a map of simplicial sets, and suppose
F\colon X\to\mathcal{C}
is a map of simplicial sets such that
\mathcal{C}
is a quasicategory. The functor
F
admits a left Kan extension along
f
if and only if for all objects
x\in X
, the colimit of the functor

X_{/y}\overset{\pi}{\to}X\overset{F}{\to}\mathcal{C}

exists in
\mathcal{C}
.

The following is a combination of special cases of
[
11
, Proposition 7.3.1.15]
and
[
11
, Corollary 7.3.1.16]

Example 2.1.8
.

For any homotopy-commuting diagram of simplicial sets

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}

where
X
,
Y
, and
\mathcal{C}
are
\infty
-categories and
f\colon X\to Y
is a categorical equivalence,
\eta
exhibits
G
as a left Kan extension of
F
along
f
.

The following guarantees existence of global left Kan extensions.

Theorem 2.1.9

(
[
11
, Corollary 7.3.6.3]
)
.

Let
f\colon X\to Y
be a map of simplicial sets, and suppose that
\mathcal{C}
is a quasicategory which admits
X_{/y}
-shaped colimits for all
y\in Y
. Then the restriction functor
f^{*}\colon\mathrm{Fun}(Y,\mathcal{C})\to\mathrm{Fun}(X,\mathcal{C})
admits a left adjoint
f_{!}
, sending a functor
F\colon X\to\mathcal{C}
to
f_{!}F\colon Y\to\mathcal{C}
, its left Kan extension along
f
.

The following is an easy consequence of
[
11
, Remark 7.3.1.11]
, to be explained later.

Proposition 2.1.10
.

Let
f\colon X\to Y
be a map of simplicial sets, and let
\mathcal{C}
be a category such that all functors
X\to\mathcal{C}
admit left Kan extensions along
f
, so that we have an adjunction
f_{!}\vdash f^{*}
. Let
G\colon Y\to\mathcal{C}
and
\eta\colon F\Rightarrow G\circ f
. The natural transformation
\eta
exhibits
G
as a left Kan extension of
F
along
f
if and only if it is adjunct (in the sense of
Definition
2.3.4
) to an equivalence in
\mathrm{Fun}(Y,\mathcal{C})
relative to
s=\mathrm{id}_{\Delta^{1}}
.

Proposition 2.1.11

(
[
11
, Proposition 7.3.7.18]
)
.

Consider
\infty
-categories and functors

\hbox to202.31pt{\vbox to79.76pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 101.15262pt\lower-39.2292pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{54.6435pt}{-7.1361pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}},

and natural transformations
\alpha\colon F\Rightarrow G\circ f
and
\beta\colon H\circ g\Rightarrow G
, and suppose that
\alpha
exhibits
G
as the left Kan extension of
F
along
f
. Then
\beta
exhibits
H
as the left Kan extension of
G
along
g
if and only if
\beta f\circ\alpha
exhibits
H
as the left Kan extension of
F
along
g\circ f
.

Proposition 2.1.12

(
[
11
, Remark 7.3.1.12]
)
.

Suppose
H\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
is an equivalence of categories,
f\colon X\to Y
is a map of simplicial sets,
G\colon Y\to\mathcal{C}
is a functor, and
\eta\colon F\Rightarrow G\circ f
is a natural transformation. Then
\eta
exhibits
G
as a left Kan extension of
F
along
f
if and only if
H\eta\colon H\circ F\Rightarrow H\circ G\circ f
exhibits
H\circ G
as a left Kan extension of
H\circ F
along
f
.

Proposition 2.1.13

(
[
11
, Remark 7.3.1.9]
)
.

The property that
\eta
exhibits
G
as a left Kan extension of
F
along
f
is homotopy-invariant; any homotopic
\eta
will do just as well.

2.2
Filling cubes relative to their boundaries

Our main goal in this
Section
2
is to prove
Theorem
2.0.2
. To do this, we must understand lifting problems of the form

\hbox to120.47pt{\vbox to53.23pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 60.23624pt\lower-26.61227pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-60.23624pt}{-21.147pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{
{}{}{}}{}{
{}{}{}}
{{{{{}}{
{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{}{{{{{}}{
{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{{}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -10.24 23.96 L 21.86 23.96}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}{{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{16.00154pt}{17.31253pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 3.32 C -2.35 1.33 -1.18 0.39 0 0 C -1.18 -0.39 -2.35 -1.33 -2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

The category
\mathbbm{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}
is defined to be the homotopy coherent nerve of the
\mathcal{S}\mathrm{et}_{\Delta}^{+}
-enriched category
\mathbf{QCat}
of quasicategories. Therefore, solving such lifting problems is equivalent to solving the adjunct lifting problems

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{C}[\Lambda^{n+1}_{0}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{QCat}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{C}[\Delta^{n+1}]}

where the diagram in question is now a diagram of
\mathcal{S}\mathrm{et}_{\Delta}^{+}
-enriched categories (where the mapping spaces of
\mathfrak{C}[\Lambda^{n+1}_{0}]
and
\mathfrak{C}[\Delta^{n+1}]
are taken to carry the flat marking). In order to solve such lifting problems, we need to fill the missing data in each mapping space. As we will see in
Subsection
2.4
, these fillings take the form of filling a cube
(\Delta^{1})^{n}
relative to its boundary missing one face.

In this subsection, we give the combinatorics of filling the
n
-cube. Our main result is
Proposition
2.2.21
, which writes the inclusion the boundary of the
n
-cube missing a certain face into the full cube as a composition of an inner anodyne map and a marked anodyne map.

Definition 2.2.1
.

The
n
-cube
is the simplicial set
C^{n}:=(\Delta^{1})^{n}
.

Note 2.2.2
.

We will consider the the factors
\Delta^{1}
of
C^{n}
to be ordered, so that we can speak about the first factor, the second factor, etc.

Our first step is to understand the nondegenerate simplices of the
n
-cube.

Definition 2.2.3
.

Denote by
a^{i}\colon\Delta^{n}\to\Delta^{1}
the map which sends
\Delta^{\{0,\ldots,i-1\}}
to
\{0\}
, and
\Delta^{\{i,\ldots,n\}}
to
\{1\}
.

Note 2.2.4
.

The superscript of
a^{i}
counts how many vertices of
\Delta^{n}
are sent to
\Delta^{\{0\}}\subset\Delta^{1}
.

Every nondegenerate simplex
\Delta^{n}\to C^{n}
can be specified by giving a walk along the edges of
C^{n}
starting at
(0,\ldots,0)
and ending at
(1,\ldots,1)
, and any simplex specified in this way is nondegenerate. We now use this to define a bijection between
S_{n}
, the symmetric group on the set
\{1,\ldots,n\}
, and the nondegenerate simplices
\Delta^{n}\to C^{n}
as follows.

Definition 2.2.5
.

For any permutation
\tau\colon\{1,\ldots,n\}\to\{1,\ldots,n\}
, we define an
n
-simplex

\phi(\tau)\colon\Delta^{n}\to C^{n};\qquad\phi(\tau)_{i}=a^{\tau(i)}.

That is,

\phi(\tau)=(a^{\tau(1)},\ldots,a^{\tau(n)}).

Note 2.2.6
.

It is easy to check that the above definition really results in a bijection between
S_{n}
and the nondegenerate simplices of
C^{n}
.

Notation 2.2.7
.

We will denote any permutatation
\tau\colon\{1,\ldots,n\}\to\{1,\ldots,n\}
by the corresponding
n
-tuple
(\tau(1),\ldots,\tau(n))
. Thus, the identity permutation is
(1,\ldots,n)
, and the permutation
\gamma_{1,2}
which swaps
1
and
2
is
(2,1,3,\ldots,n)
.

Given a permutation
\tau\in S_{n}
corresponding to an
n
-simplex

\phi(\tau)=(a^{\tau(1)},\ldots,a^{\tau(n)})\colon\Delta^{n}\to C^{n},

the
i
th face of
\phi(\tau)
is a nondegenerate
(n-1)
-simplex in
C^{n}
, i.e. a map
\Delta^{n-1}\to C^{n}
. We calculate this as follows: for any
a^{i}\colon\Delta^{n}\to\Delta^{1}
and any face map
\partial_{j}\colon\Delta^{n-1}\to\Delta^{n}
, we note that

\partial_{j}^{*}a^{i}=\begin{cases}a^{i-1},&j<i\\
a^{i},&j\geq i\end{cases},

where by minor abuse of notation we denote the map
\Delta^{n-1}\to\Delta^{1}
sending
\Delta^{\{0,\ldots i-1\}}
to
\{0\}
and the rest to
\{1\}
also by
a^{i}
. We then have that

\displaystyle d_{i}\tau=d_{i}(a^{\tau(1)},\ldots,a^{\tau(n)}):=(\partial_{i}^{*}a^{\tau(1)},\ldots,\partial_{i}^{*}a^{\tau(n)}).

Example 2.2.8
.

Consider the 3-simplex
(a^{1},a^{2},a^{3})
in
C^{3}
. This has spine

(0,0,0)\to(1,0,0)\to(1,1,0)\to(1,1,1),

as is easy to see quickly:

•

The function
\Delta^{3}\to\Delta^{1}
corresponding to the first coordinate is
a^{1}
, so the first coordinate of the first vertex is 0, and the first coordinate of the rest of the vertices are 1.

•

The function
\Delta^{3}\to\Delta^{1}
corresponding to the second coordinate is
a^{2}
, so the second coordinates of the first two points are equal to zero, and the second coordinate of the remaining vertices is 1.

•

The function
\Delta^{3}\to\Delta^{1}
corresponding to the third coordinate is
a^{3}
, so the third coordinates of the first three points is equal to zero, and the second coordinate of the final vertex is equal to 1.

Using
Equation
2.2
, we then calculate that

d_{2}(a^{1},a^{2},a^{3})=(a^{1},a^{2},a^{2}).

This corresponds to the 2-simplex in
C^{n}
with spine

(0,0,0)\to(1,0,0)\to(1,1,1).

Our next task is to understand the boundary of the
n
-cube. We can view the boundary of the cube
C^{n}
as the union of its faces.

Definition 2.2.9
.

The
boundary of the
n
-cube
is the simplicial subset

\partial C^{n}:=\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}\bigcup_{j=0}^{1}\overset{(1)}{\Delta^{1}}\times\cdots\times\overset{(i)}{\{j\}}\times\cdots\times\overset{(n)}{\Delta^{1}}\subset C^{n}.

(5)

The following is easy to see.

Proposition 2.2.10
.

An
(n-1)
-simplex

\gamma=(\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{n})\colon\Delta^{n-1}\to C^{n}

lies entirely within the face

\overset{(1)}{\Delta^{1}}\times\cdots\times\overset{(i)}{\{0\}}\times\cdots\times\overset{(n)}{\Delta^{1}}

if and only if
\gamma_{i}=a^{n}=\mathrm{const}_{0}
, and to the face

\overset{(1)}{\Delta^{1}}\times\cdots\times\overset{(i)}{\{1\}}\times\cdots\times\overset{(n)}{\Delta^{1}}

if and only if
\gamma_{i}=a^{0}=\mathrm{const}_{1}
.

Definition 2.2.11
.

The
left box of the
n
-cube
, denoted
LC^{n}
, is the simplicial subset of
C^{n}
given by the union of all of the faces in
Equation
5
except for

\Delta^{1}\times\cdots\times\Delta^{1}\times\Delta^{\{1\}}.

Note that drawing the box with the final coordinate going from left to right, the right face is open here; the terminology is chosen to match with ‘left horn’.

Example 2.2.12
.

The simplicial subset
LC^{2}\subset C^{2}
can be drawn as follows.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(0,0)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(0,1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(1,0)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(1,1)}}

Note that in
LC^{2}
, the morphisms
(0,0)\to(1,0)\to(1,1)
form an inner horn, which we can fill by pushing out along an inner horn inclusion. Our main goal in this section is to show that this is generically true: given a left cube
LC^{n}
, we can fill much of
C^{n}
using pushouts along inner horn inclusions. To this end, it will be helpful to know how each nondegenerate simplex in
C^{n}
intersects
LC^{n}
.

Lemma 2.2.13
.

Let
\phi\colon\Delta^{n}\to C^{n}
be a nondegenerate
n
-simplex corresponding to the permutaton
\tau\in S_{n}
. We have the following.

1.

The zeroth face
d_{0}\phi
belongs to
LC^{n}
if and only if
\tau(n)\neq 1
.

2.

For
0<i<n
, the face
d_{i}\phi
never belongs to
LC^{n}
.

3.

The
n
th face
d_{n}\phi
always belongs to
LC^{n}
.

Proof.

1.

We have

d_{0}\phi=(a^{\tau(0)-1},\ldots a^{\tau(n)-1})

since
\tau(i)>0
for all
i
. For
j=\tau^{-1}(1)
, we have that
\tau(j)=1
, so the
j
th entry of
d_{0}\phi
is
a^{0}
. Thus,
Proposition
2.2.10
guarantees that
d_{0}\phi
is contained in the face

\overset{(1)}{\Delta^{1}}\times\cdots\times\overset{(j)}{\{1\}}\times\cdots\times\overset{(n)}{\Delta^{1}}.

This face belongs to
LC^{n}
except when
j=n
.

2.

In this case, the superscript of each entry of
d_{i}\phi
is between
1
and
n-1
, hence not equal to
n
or
0
.

3.

In this case,

d_{n}(a^{\tau(1)},\ldots,a^{\tau(n)})=(a^{\tau(1)},\ldots,a^{\tau(n)})

since
n\geq\tau(i)
for all
1\leq i\leq n
. Thus, the
\tau^{-1}(n)=j
th entry is
a^{n}
, so
d_{n}\phi
belongs to the face

\overset{(1)}{\Delta^{1}}\times\cdots\times\overset{(j)}{\{0\}}\times\cdots\times\overset{(n)}{\Delta^{1}}.

∎

This is promising: it means that for many simplices in
C^{n}
which we want to fill relative to
LC^{n}
, we already have the data of the first and last faces. The following lemma will allow us to take advantage of this.

Notation 2.2.14
.

For any subset
T\subseteq[n]
, write

\Lambda^{n}_{T}:=\bigcup_{t\in T}d_{t}\Delta^{n}\subset\Delta^{n}.

Lemma 2.2.15
.

For any proper subset
T\subset[n]
containing 0 and
n
, the inclusion
\Lambda^{n}_{T}\hookrightarrow\Delta^{n}
is inner anodyne.

Proof.

Induction. For
n=2
,
T
must be equal to
\{0,2\}
, so
\Delta^{2}_{T}=\Lambda^{2}_{1}
.

Assume the result holds for
n-1
, and let
T\subset[n]
be a proper subset containing
0
and
n
. Using the inductive step, we can fill all but one of the faces
d_{1}\Delta^{n},\ldots,d_{n-1}\Delta^{n}
. The result follows
∎

We can use
Lemma
2.2.15
to show that we can fill much of
C^{n}
as a sequence of inner anodyne pushouts, but to do this we need to pick an order in which to fill our simplices. We do this as follows.

Definition 2.2.16
.

We define a total order on
S_{n}
as follows. For any two permutations
\tau
,
\tau^{\prime}\in S_{n}
, we say that
\tau<\tau^{\prime}
if there exists
k\in[n]
such that the following conditions are satisfied.

•

For all
i<k
, we have that
\tau^{-1}(i)=\tau^{\prime-1}(i)
.

•

We have that
\tau^{-1}(k)<\tau^{\prime-1}(k)
.

We then define a total order on the nondegenerate simplices of
C^{n}
by saying that
\phi(\tau)<\phi(\tau^{\prime})
if and only if
\tau<\tau^{\prime}
.

Note that this is
not
the lexicographic order on
S_{n}
; instead, we have that
\tau<\tau^{\prime}
if and only if
\tau^{-1}
is less than
\tau^{\prime-1}
under the lexicographic order.

Example 2.2.17
.

The elements of the permutation group
S_{3}
have the order

(1,2,3)<(1,3,2)<(2,1,3)<(3,1,2)<(2,3,1)<(3,2,1).

We use this ordering to define our filtration.

Notation 2.2.18
.

For
\tau\in S_{n}
, we mean by

\bigcup_{\tau^{\prime}\in S_{n}}^{\tau}

the union over all
\tau^{\prime}\in S_{n}
for which
\tau^{\prime}<\tau
with respect to the ordering defined above. Note the strict inequality.

We would like to show that each step of the filtration

\displaystyle\begin{split}LC^{n}&\hookrightarrow LC^{n}\cup\phi(1,\ldots,n)\\
&\hookrightarrow\cdots\\
&\hookrightarrow LC^{n}\cup\bigcup_{\tau\in S_{n}}^{(2,\ldots,n,1)}\phi(\tau)\end{split}

(6)

is inner anodyne. To do this, it suffices to show that for each
\tau<(2,\ldots,n,1)
, the intersection

B_{\tau}=\left(LC^{n}\cup\bigcup_{\tau^{\prime}\in S_{n}}^{\tau}\phi(\tau^{\prime})\right)\cap\phi(\tau)

(7)

is of the form
\Lambda^{n}_{T}
for some proper
T\subseteq[n]
containing 0 and
n
.

Proposition 2.2.19
.

Each inclusion in
Equation
6
is inner anodyne.

Proof.

The first inclusion

LC^{n}\hookrightarrow LC^{n}\cup\phi(1,\ldots,n)

is inner anodyne because by
Lemma
2.2.13
the intersection
LC^{n}\cap\phi(1,\ldots,n)
is of the form
d_{0}\Delta^{n}\cup d_{n}\Delta^{n}
.

Each subsequent intersection contains the faces
d_{0}\Delta^{n}
and
d_{n}\Delta^{n}
(again by
Lemma
2.2.13
), so by
Lemma
2.2.15
, it suffices to show that for each
\tau
under consideration, there is at least one face not shared with any previous
\tau^{\prime}
.

To this end, fix
0<i<n
, and consider
\tau\in S_{n}
such that
\tau\neq(1,\ldots,n)
. We consider the face
d_{i}\phi(\tau)
. Let
j=\tau^{-1}(i)
and
j^{\prime}=\tau^{-1}(i+1)
. Then
d_{i}a^{\tau(j)}=d_{i}a^{\tau(j^{\prime})}
, so
d_{i}\phi(\tau)=d_{i}\phi(\tau\circ\gamma_{jj^{\prime}})
, where
\gamma_{jj^{\prime}}\in S_{n}
is the permutation swapping
j
and
j^{\prime}
. Thus, the face
d_{i}\sigma(\tau)
is equal to the face
d_{i}\sigma(\tau\circ\gamma_{jj^{\prime}})
, which is already contained in the union under consideration if and only
\tau\circ\gamma_{jj^{\prime}}<\tau
. This in turn is true if and only if
j<j^{\prime}
.

Assume that every face of
\phi(\tau)
is contained in the union. Then

\tau^{-1}(1)<\tau^{-1}(2)<\cdots\tau^{-1}(n),

which implies that
\tau=(1,\ldots,n)
. This is a contradiction. Thus, each boundary inclusion under consideration is of the form
\Lambda^{n}_{T}\hookrightarrow\Delta^{n}
, for
T\subset[n]
a proper subset containing 0 and
n
, and is thus inner anodyne.
∎

Each nondegenerate simplex of
C^{n}
which we have yet to fill is of the form
\phi(\tau)
, where
\tau(n)=1
. Thus, each of their spines begins

(0,\ldots,0,0)\to(0,\ldots,0,1)\to\cdots,

and then remains confined to the face
\Delta^{1}\times\cdots\times\Delta^{1}\times\{1\}
. This implies that the part of
LC^{n}
yet to be filled is of the form
\Delta^{0}\ast C^{n-1}
, and the boundary of this with respect to which we must do the filling is of the form
\Delta^{0}\ast\partial C^{n-1}
.

Our next task is to show that we can also perform this filling, under the assumption that
(0,\ldots,0,0)\to(0,\ldots,0,1)
is marked. We do not give the details of this proof, at is quite similar to the proof of
Proposition
2.2.19
. One continues to fill simplices in the order prescribed in
Definition
2.2.16
, and shows that each of these inclusions is marked anodyne. The main tool is the following lemma, proved using the same inductive argument as
Lemma
2.2.15
.

Lemma 2.2.20
.

For any subset
T\subset[n]
containing 1 and
n
, and
not
containing 0, the inclusion

(\Lambda^{n}_{T},\mathcal{E})\hookrightarrow(\Delta^{n},\mathcal{F})

is (cocartesian-)marked anodyne, where by
\mathcal{F}
we mean the set of degenerate
1
-simplces together with
\Delta^{\{0,1\}}
, and by
\mathcal{E}
we mean the restriction of this marking to
\Lambda^{n}_{T}
.

We collect our major results from this section in the following proposition.

Proposition 2.2.21
.

Denote by
J^{n}\subseteq C^{n}
the simplicial subset spanned by those nondegenerate
n
-simplices whose spines do not begin
(0,\ldots,0,0)\to(0,\ldots,0,1)
. We can write the filling
LC^{n}\hookrightarrow C^{n}
as a composition

where

where the top inclusion underlies a marked anodyne morphism

(\Delta^{0}\ast\partial C^{n-1},\mathcal{G})\hookrightarrow(\Delta^{0}\ast C^{n-1},\mathcal{G}),

where the marking
\mathcal{G}
contains all degenerate morphisms together with the morphism
(0,\ldots,0,0)\to(0,\ldots,0,1)
.

2.3
Adjunct data

When confronted with a lifting problem, one frequently finds a lift by passing to an adjoint lifting problem which has a solution, and transporting the solution back along the adjunction. This relies on the fact that providing data on one side of an adjunction is, in a certain sense, equivalent to providing adjunct data on the other side. In this section, we give a formalization of the the notion of adjunct data in the
\infty
-categorical context. Our main result is
Proposition
2.3.9
, which shows that under certain conditions, we can use data on one side of an adjunction of an
\infty
-categories to fill in data on the other side. First, we recall somewhat explicitly the definition of an adjunction given in
[
10
]
.

Definition 2.3.1
.

An
adjunction
is a bicartesian fibration
p\colon\mathcal{M}\to\Delta^{1}
. We say that
p
is
associated
to functors
f\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
and
g\colon\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{C}
if there exist equivalences
h_{0}\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{M}_{0}
and
h_{1}\colon\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{M}_{1}
, and a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}\times\Delta^{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}\times\Delta^{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta^{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{pr}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{pr}}

such that the following conditions are satisfied.

•

The map
u
is associated to the functor
f
:
the restriction
u|\mathcal{C}\times\{0\}=h_{0}
, the restriction
u|\mathcal{C}\times\{1\}=f\circ h_{1}
, and for each
c\in\mathcal{C}
, the edge
u|\{c\}\times\Delta^{1}
is
p
-cocartesian.

•

The map
v
is associated to the functor
g
:
the restriction
v|\mathcal{D}\times\{0\}=g\circ h_{0}
, the restriction
u|\mathcal{D}\times\{1\}=h_{1}
, and for each
d\in\mathcal{D}
, the edge
u|\{d\}\times\Delta^{1}
is
p
-cartesian.

If
f
and
g
are functors to which an adjunction is associated as above, then we say that
f
is left adjoint to
g
, and equivalently that
g
is right adjoint to
f
.

Note 2.3.2
.

As noted in
[
10
]
, this terminology is slightly imprecise; it would be more correct to say that
p
is associated to
f
and
g
via the data
(h_{0},h_{1},u,v)
.

Note 2.3.3
.

It is shown in
[
10
]
that if
f
and
g
are functors such that
f
is left adjoint to
g
, then we can choose an adjunction
p\colon\mathcal{M}\to\Delta^{1}
and data
(h_{0},h_{1},u,v)
such that
h_{0}
and
h_{1}
are isomorphisms. We can use this to identify
\mathcal{M}_{0}
with
\mathcal{C}
, and
\mathcal{M}_{1}
with
\mathcal{D}
. In what follows we will always assume that we have chosen such data, and will thus leave the isomorphisms
h_{0}
and
h_{1}
implicit.

Definition 2.3.4
.

Let
s\colon K\to\Delta^{1}
be a map of simplicial sets whose fibers we denote by
K_{0}
and
K_{1}
, and let
p\colon\mathcal{M}\to\Delta^{1}
be a bicartesian fibration associated to adjoint functors

f:\mathcal{C}\longleftrightarrow\mathcal{D}:g

via data
(u\colon\mathcal{C}\times\Delta^{1}\to\mathcal{M},v\colon\mathcal{D}\times\Delta^{1}\to\mathcal{M})
. We say that a map
\alpha\colon K\to\mathcal{C}
is
adjunct
to a map
\tilde{\alpha}\colon K\to\mathcal{D}
relative to
s
, and equivalently that
\tilde{\alpha}
is adjunct to
\alpha
relative to
s
, if there exists a map
A\colon K\times\Delta^{1}\to\mathcal{M}
such that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K\times\Delta^{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta^{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{pr}_{\Delta^{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}

commutes, and such that the following conditions are satisfied.

1.

The restriction
A|_{K\times\{0\}}=\alpha
.

2.

The restriction
A|_{K\times\{1\}}=\tilde{\alpha}
.

3.

The restriction
A|K_{0}\times\Delta^{1}
is equal to the composition

K_{0}\times\Delta^{1}\hookrightarrow K\times\Delta^{1}\overset{\alpha\circ\times\mathrm{id}}{\to}\mathcal{C}\times\Delta^{1}\overset{u}{\to}\mathcal{M}.

4.

The restriction
A|K_{1}\times\Delta^{1}
is equal to the composition

K_{1}\times\Delta^{1}\hookrightarrow K\times\Delta^{1}\overset{\tilde{\alpha}\times\mathrm{id}}{\to}\mathcal{D}\times\Delta^{1}\overset{v}{\to}\mathcal{M}.

In the next examples, fix adjoint functors

f:\mathcal{C}\longleftrightarrow\mathcal{D}:g

corresponding to a bicartesian fibration
p\colon\mathcal{M}\to\Delta^{1}
via data
(u,v)
.

Example 2.3.5
.

Any morphism in
\mathcal{D}
of the form
fC\to D
is adjunct to some morphism in
\mathcal{C}
of the form
C\to gD
, witnessed by any square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces fC}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces gD}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}

in
\mathcal{M}
where
a
is
u|\{c\}\times\Delta^{1}
and
b
is
v|\{d\}\times\Delta^{1}
. This corresponds to the map
s=\mathrm{id}\colon\Delta^{1}\to\Delta^{1}
.

Example 2.3.6
.

Pick some object
D\in\mathcal{D}
, and consider the identity morphism
\mathrm{id}\colon gD\to gD
in
\mathcal{C}
. This morphism is adjunct to the component of the unit map
\eta_{D}\colon D\to fgD
relative to
s=\mathrm{id}\colon\Delta^{1}\to\Delta^{1}
.

Example 2.3.7
.

For any simplicial sets
K
and
K^{\prime}
, any diagram
K\ast K^{\prime}\to\mathcal{D}
such that
K
is in the image of
f
is adjunct to some diagram
K\ast K^{\prime}\to\mathcal{C}
such that
K^{\prime}
is in the image of
g
. This will follow from
Proposition
2.3.9
.

Lemma 2.3.8
.

The inclusion of marked simplicial sets

\left(\Delta^{n}\times\Delta^{\{0\}}\coprod_{\partial\Delta^{n}\times\Delta^{\{0\}}}\partial\Delta^{n}\times\Delta^{1},\mathcal{E}\right)\hookrightarrow(\Delta^{n}\times\Delta^{1},\mathcal{F})

(8)

where the marking
\mathcal{F}
is the flat marking together with the edge
\Delta^{\{0\}}\times\Delta^{1}
, and
\mathcal{E}
is the restriction of this marking, is marked (cocartesian) anodyne.

The next proposition shows the power of adjunctions in lifting problems: given data on either side of an adjunction, we can fill in adjunct data on the other side.

Proposition 2.3.9
.

Let
\mathcal{M}\to\Delta^{1}
,
\mathcal{C}
,
\mathcal{D}
,
f
,
g
,
u
and
v
be as in
Definition
2.3.1
, and let

(9)

such that
\tilde{\alpha}
is adjunct to
\alpha
relative to
s
. Furthermore, any two such lifts are equivalent as functors
K\to\mathcal{D}
.

Proof.

and
\alpha^{\prime}

and the map

where
(K\times\Delta^{1})^{\heartsuit}
is the marked simplicial set where the only nondenerate morphisms marked are those of the form
\{k\}\times\Delta^{1}
for
k\in K|_{s^{-1}\{0\}}
, and the
\heartsuit
-marked pushout-product denotes the restriction of this marking to the pushout-product. We can write the morphism of simplicial sets underlying
w
as a smash-product
(K^{\prime}\hookrightarrow K)\wedge(\Delta^{\{0\}}\hookrightarrow\Delta^{1})
. Building
K^{\prime}\hookrightarrow K
simplex by simplex, in increasing order of dimension, we can write
w
as a transfinite composition of pushouts along inclusions of the form given in
Equation
8
(by assumption each simplex
\sigma
we adjoin to
K
is not completely contained in
K^{\prime}
, so the initial vertex of
\sigma
must lie in the fiber of
s
over
0
). We can thus also build our lift
\ell
by lifting against each of these in turn, and we can always choose each lift so that it is compatible with
v
. We can then take
\tilde{\alpha}=\ell|K\times\{1\}
.

It is manifest from this technique that the space of lifts is contractible; in particular, any two such lifts are equivalent in
\mathrm{Map}(K\times\Delta^{1},\mathcal{M})
, so their restrictions to
K\times\{1\}
are equivalent as functors
K\to\mathcal{D}
.

∎

Example 2.3.10
.

Let
f:\mathcal{C}\leftrightarrow\mathcal{D}:g
be an adjunction of

where the solid diagrams in each category are adjunct to one another.

Proposition
2.3.9
implies that the solution to the lifting problem on the left yields one on the right. Similarly, its dual implies the converse. Thus,
Proposition
2.3.9
implies in particular that such lifting problems are equivalent.

We note that in the proof of
Proposition
2.3.9
, we did not need to use the full power of the statement that
f
was left adjoint to
g
(i.e. that
p\colon\mathcal{M}\to\Delta^{1}
was a bicartesian fibration), only that
f
was
locally
left adjoint to
g
, (i.e. that
p
admitted cocartesian lifts at certain objects). This allows us to generalize
Proposition
2.3.9
somewhat.

Definition 2.3.11
.

Let
g\colon\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{C}
be a functor between quasicategories, and pick some cartesian fibration
p\colon\mathcal{M}\to\Delta^{1}
which classifies it. We assume without loss of generality that
h_{0}\colon\mathcal{M}_{0}\cong\mathcal{C}
and
h_{1}\colon\mathcal{M}_{1}\cong\mathcal{D}
are isomorphisms, and we will notationally suppress them.

We say that
g

admits a left adjoint at
c\in\mathcal{C}
if
p
admits a cocartesian lift of the morphism
\mathrm{id}_{\Delta^{1}}
with source
c\in\mathcal{M}_{0}
.

Definition 2.3.12
.

Let
s\colon K\to\Delta^{1}
be a map of simplicial sets whose fibers we denote by
K_{0}
and
K_{1}
, and let
p\colon\mathcal{M}\to\Delta^{1}
be a cartesian fibration associated to
f\colon\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{C}
via a map
v\colon\mathcal{D}\times\Delta^{1}\to\mathcal{M}
. We say that a map
\alpha\colon K\to\mathcal{C}
is
adjunct
to a map
\tilde{\alpha}\colon K\to\mathcal{D}
relative to
s
, and equivalently that
\tilde{\alpha}
is adjunct to
\alpha
relative to
s
, if there exists a map
A\colon K\times\Delta^{1}\to\mathcal{M}
such that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K\times\Delta^{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta^{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{pr}_{\Delta^{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}

commutes, and such that the following conditions are satisfied.

1.

The restriction
A|_{K\times\{0\}}=\alpha
.

2.

The restriction
A|_{K\times\{1\}}=\tilde{\alpha}
.

3.

For each vertex
k\in K_{0}
, the image of
\{k\}\times\Delta^{1}
under
A
in
\mathcal{M}
is
p
-cartesian.

4.

The restriction
A|K_{1}\times\Delta^{1}
is equal to the composition

K_{1}\times\Delta^{1}\hookrightarrow K\times\Delta^{1}\overset{\tilde{\alpha}\times\mathrm{id}}{\to}\mathcal{D}\times\Delta^{1}\overset{v}{\to}\mathcal{M}.

Example 2.3.13
.

Suppose
f\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
and
F\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
are functors. The statement that the left Kan extension
f_{!}F\colon\mathcal{C^{\prime}}\to\mathcal{D}
exists is equivalent to the statement that
f^{*}
admits a left adjoint at
F
.

The proof of
Proposition
2.3.9
can be used as is to show the following statement.

(11)

We expect that the reader is familiar with the basics of (scaled) rigidification, so we give only a rough description of this lifting problem here. The objects of the simplicial category
\mathfrak{C}[\Delta^{n+1}]
are given by the set
\{0,\ldots,n+1\}
, and the mapping spaces are defined by

\mathfrak{C}[\Delta^{n+1}](i,j)=\begin{cases}N(P_{ij}),&i\leq j\\
\emptyset,&i>j\end{cases}.

where
P_{ij}
is the poset of subsets of the linearly ordered set
\{i,\ldots,j\}
containing
i
and
j
, ordered by inclusion. The simplicial category
\mathfrak{C}[\Lambda^{n+1}_{0}]
has the same objects as
\mathfrak{C}[\Delta^{n+1}]
, and each morphism space
\mathfrak{C}[\Lambda^{n+1}_{0}](i,j)
is a simplicial subset of
\mathfrak{C}[\Delta^{n+1}](i,j)
, as we shall soon describe.

The map
\mathfrak{C}[\Lambda^{n+1}_{0}](i,j)\hookrightarrow\mathfrak{C}[\Delta^{n+1}](i,j)
is an isomorphism except for
(i,j)=(1,n+1)
and
(i,j)=(0,n+1)
; in these cases, it is an inclusion. The missing data corresponds in the case of
(1,n+1)
to the missing face
d_{0}\Delta^{n+1}
of
\Lambda^{n+1}_{0}
, and in the case of
(0,n+1)
to the missing interior. Thus, to find a filling as in
Equation
11
, we need to solve the lifting problems

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{C}[\Lambda^{n+1}_{0}](1,n+1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Fun}(\mathcal{F}(1),\mathcal{F}(n+1))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{C}[\Delta^{n+1}](1,n+1)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell^{\prime}}

(13)

However, these problems are not independent; the filling
\ell
of the full simplex needs to agree with the filling
\ell^{\prime}
we found for the missing face of the horn, corresponding to the condition that the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{C}[\Delta^{n+1}](1,n+1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Fun}(\mathcal{F}(1),\mathcal{F}(n+1))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{C}[\Delta^{n+1}](0,n+1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Fun}(\mathcal{F}(0),\mathcal{F}(n+1))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\{0,1\}}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}({\{0,1\}})^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell}

(14)

commute.

Notation 2.4.1
.

Recall our desired filling
\mathcal{F}\colon\mathfrak{C}[\Delta^{n+1}]\to\mathbf{QCat}
of
Equation
11
. We will denote
\mathcal{F}(i)
by
X_{i}
, and for each subset
S=\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}\}\subseteq[n]
, we will denote
\mathcal{F}(S)\in\mathrm{Map}(X_{i_{1}},X_{i_{k}})
by
f_{i_{k}\cdots i_{1}}
. For any inclusion
S^{\prime}\subseteq S\subseteq[n]
preserving minimim and maximum elements, we will denote the corresponding morphism by
\alpha^{S^{\prime}}_{S}
.

Theorem 2.4.2
.

For any
n\geq 2
and any globally left Kan
2
-simplex
\tau
, the solid extension problem

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta^{\{0,1,n+1\}}_{\flat}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\Lambda^{n+1}_{0})_{\flat}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbbm{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta^{n+1}_{\flat}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau}

admits a dashed filler.

Note 2.4.3
.

The author would like to offer the friendly recommendation that in reading the proof below, it is helpful to follow along with the explanation given at the beginning of
Section
2
.

Proof.

We need to solve the lifting problems of
Equation
12
and
Equation
13
, and check that our solutions satisfy the condition of
Equation
14
. In the discussuion surrounding these equations, we ignored that the simplex
\tau\colon\Delta^{\{0,1,n+1\}}_{\flat}\to\mathbbm{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}
was left Kan. We now reintroduce this information. The information that the simplex
\tau\colon\Delta^{\{0,1,n+1\}}_{\flat}\to\mathbbm{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}
is left Kan tells us the following.

•

Concretely, it tells us that the map
f_{n,1}
is the left Kan extension of
f_{n,0}
along
f_{1,0}
, and that
\alpha_{\{0,n+1\}}^{\{0,1,n+1\}}\colon f_{n+1,0}\to f_{n+1,1}\circ f_{1,0}
is the unit map.

•

In particular,
Example
2.3.13
then tells us that the map
f_{1,0}^{*}
admits a left adjoint at
f_{n+1,0}
.

The lifting problems as described above are somewhat unwieldy, given in terms of mapping spaces of simplicial categories
\mathfrak{C}[\Delta^{n+1}]
and
\mathfrak{C}[\Lambda^{n+1}_{0}]
. It will be useful to give these mapping spaces more down-to-earth descriptions, in terms of simplicial sets with which we are familiar. We have the following succinct descriptions of the inclusions of
Equation
12
and
Equation
13
along which we have to extend.

•

There is an isomorphism of simplicial sets

\mathfrak{C}[\Delta^{n+1}](0,n+1)\overset{\cong}{\to}C^{n}

specified completely by sending a subset
S\subseteq[n+1]
to the point

(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})\in C^{n},\qquad z_{i}=\begin{cases}1,&i\in S\\
0,&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

The inclusion
\mathfrak{C}[\Lambda^{n+1}_{0}](0,n+1)\hookrightarrow\mathfrak{C}[\Delta^{n+1}](0,n+1)
corresponds to the simplicial subset
LC^{n}\hookrightarrow C^{n}
.

•

There is a similar isomorphism of simplicial sets

\mathfrak{C}[\Delta^{n+1}](1,n+1)\overset{\cong}{\to}C^{n-1}

specified completely by sending
S\subseteq\{1,\ldots,n+1\}
to the point

(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n-1})\in C^{n-1},\qquad z_{i}=\begin{cases}1,&i+1\in S\\
0,&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

The inclusion
\mathfrak{C}[\Lambda^{n+1}_{0}](1,n+1)\hookrightarrow\mathfrak{C}[\Delta^{n+1}](1,n+1)
corresponds to the inclusion
\partial C^{n-1}\hookrightarrow C^{n-1}
.

Using these descriptions, we can write our lifting problems in the more inviting form

The image of the 1-simplex
(0,\ldots,0)\to(0,\ldots,1)
of
\Delta^{0}\ast\partial C^{n-1}\subseteq C^{n}
under
\alpha
is the unit map
\alpha^{\{0,1,n+1\}}_{\{0,n+1\}}\colon f_{n+1,0}\to f_{n+1,1}\circ f_{1,0}
. This is not in general an equivalence, so we cannot use
Proposition
2.2.21
to solve the lifting problem
(*^{\prime})
directly. However, the unit map is adjunct to an equivalence in
\mathrm{Fun}(X_{1},X_{n+1})
relative to
s=\mathrm{id}_{\Delta^{1}}
. Furthermore, the restriction of
\alpha
to
\partial C^{n-1}
is in the image of
f^{*}_{1,0}
, so by
Proposition
2.3.14
and our assumption that
f_{10}^{*}
admits a left adjoint at
f_{n+1,0}
, we can augment the map
\tilde{\alpha}^{\prime}
to a map
\tilde{\alpha}\colon\Delta^{0}\ast\partial C^{n-1}\to\mathrm{Fun}(X_{1},X_{n-1})
which is adjunct to
\alpha
relative to the map

s\colon\Delta^{0}\ast\partial C^{n-1}\to\Delta^{1}

sending
\Delta^{0}
to
\Delta^{\{0\}}
and
\partial C^{n-1}
to
\Delta^{\{1\}}
; in particular, the image of the morphism
(0,\ldots,0)\to(0,\ldots 1)
under
\tilde{\alpha}^{\prime}
is an equivalence. This allows us to replace the lifting problem
(**)
by the superficially more complicated lifting problem

(**^{\prime})=\hbox to175.45pt{\vbox to53.85pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 87.72351pt\lower-25.70949pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

However, since image of
(0,\ldots 0)\to(0,\ldots,1)
is an equivalence,
Proposition
2.2.21
implies that we can solve the lifting problem
(**^{\prime})
. Again using (the dual to)
Proposition
2.3.14
, we can transport this filling to a solution to the lifting problem
(*^{\prime})
. The condition
(\star)
amounts to demanding that the restriction
\beta|_{C^{n-1}}
be the image of
\tilde{\beta}|_{C^{n-1}}
under
f_{1,0}^{*}
, which is true by construction.
∎

2.5
A few other tricks with Kan extensions

We end this section with a few miscellaneous tricks which we can play with maps
\Delta^{n}\to\mathbbm{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}
involving left Kan simplices.

Lemma 2.5.1
.

Denote by
\mathcal{E}\subseteq\mathrm{Hom}(\Delta^{2},\Delta^{3})
the subset containing all degenerate
2
-simplices together with the simplices
\Delta^{\{0,2,3\}}
and
\Delta^{\{1,2,3\}}
. Let
\sigma\colon\Delta^{3}_{\mathcal{E}}\to\mathbbm{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}
such that
\Delta^{\{2,3\}}
is mapped to an equivalence. Then
\sigma|\Delta^{\{0,1,2\}}
is left Kan if and only if
\sigma|\Delta^{\{0,1,3\}}
is left Kan.

Proof.

Replacing thin simplices by strict compositions using
Proposition
2.1.13
, this statement reduces to that of
Proposition
2.1.12
.
∎

The next lemma is similar but easier.

Lemma 2.5.2
.

Denote by
\mathcal{E}\subseteq\mathrm{Hom}(\Delta^{2},\Delta^{3})
the subset containing all degenerate
2
-simplices together with the simplices
\Delta^{\{0,2,3\}}
and
\Delta^{\{1,2,3\}}
. Let
\sigma\colon\Delta^{3}_{\mathcal{E}}\to\mathbbm{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}
such that
\Delta^{\{2,3\}}
is mapped to an equivalence. Then
\sigma|\Delta^{\{0,1,2\}}
is thin if and only if
\sigma|\Delta^{\{0,1,3\}}
is thin.

Lemma 2.5.3
.

Denote by
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathrm{Hom}(\Delta^{2},\Delta^{3})
the subset containing all degenerate
2
-simplices, together with the simplex
\Delta^{\{0,1,2\}}
. Let
\sigma\colon\Delta^{3}_{\mathcal{F}}\to\mathbbm{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}
such that
\sigma|\Delta^{\{0,1,3\}}
is left Kan. Then
\sigma|\Delta^{\{0,2,3\}}
is left Kan if and only if
\sigma|\Delta^{\{1,2,3\}}
is left Kan.

Proof.

Replacing thin simplices by strict compositions using
Proposition
2.1.13
, this reduces to
Propsition
2.1.11
.
∎

3
Local systems

For any
\infty
-category
\mathcal{C}
, a
\mathcal{C}
-local system on some space
X
is simply a functor
X\to\mathcal{C}
. Thus, the
\infty
-category of
\mathcal{C}
-local systems on
X
is simply
\mathbf{LS}(\mathcal{C})_{X}:=\mathrm{Fun}(X,\mathcal{C})
. We would like to consider local systems on all spaces
X
simultaneously, defining an
\infty
-category
\mathbf{LS}(\mathcal{C})
of
\mathcal{C}
-local systems.

We follow the general strategy laid out in
[
7
]
. We will consider a cartesian fibration

p^{\prime}\colon\textstyle\int\mathrm{Fun}(-,-)\to\mathcal{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}\times\mathcal{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}^{\mathrm{op}}

which classifies the functor

\mathrm{Fun}(-,-)\colon\mathcal{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}\to\mathcal{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty};\qquad(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})\mapsto\mathrm{Fun}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D}).

The strict pullback
p
of
p^{\prime}
in the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{LS}(\mathcal{C})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\int\mathrm{Fun}(-,-)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{S}\times\{\mathcal{C}\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}\times\mathcal{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}^{\mathrm{op}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{\prime}}

will then classify the functor

\mathcal{S}\to\mathcal{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty};\qquad X\mapsto\mathbf{LS}(\mathcal{C})_{X}.

The total space
\mathbf{LS}(\mathcal{C})
of
p
will thus be our candidate for our
\infty
-category of local systems. The fibration
p
remembers the source of the local system.

In
[
6
]
, a suitable model for
\int\mathrm{Fun}(-,-)
is given: the so-called
enhanced twisted arrow category

\mathbf{Tw}(\mathbbm{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty})
. In
Subsection
3.1
, we reproduce the pertinent points of
[
6
]
, defining the enhanced twisted arrow category. In
Subsection
3.3
, we use the enhanced twisted arrow category to define the category
\mathbf{LS}(\mathcal{C})
of local systems, together with a cartesian fibration
p\colon\mathbf{LS}(\mathcal{C})\to\mathcal{S}
classifying the functor

\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathcal{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty};\qquad X\to\mathrm{Fun}(X,\mathcal{C}).

We note that this functor sends a map of spaces
f\colon X\to Y
to the pullback functor

f^{*}\colon\mathrm{Fun}(Y,\mathcal{C})\to\mathrm{Fun}(X,\mathcal{C}).

If
\mathcal{C}
is cocomplete, each functor
f^{*}
has a left adjoint
f_{!}
given by left Kan extension. By abstract nonsense, the cartesian fibration
p
is also a cocartesian fibration, whose cocartesian edges correspond to left Kan extension. In
Subsection
3.4
we will show this explicitly, using results built up in
Subsection
3.2
and
Subsection
3.3
; if the reader is willing to take this on faith, these sections can be safely skipped.

Note 3.0.1
.

The reader may notice that we are being inefficient here. If we were only interested in the construction outlined above, then using the twisted arrow category would be much more combinatorially strenuous than necessary; the objects of the twisted arrow category
\mathbf{Tw}(\mathbbm{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty})
are functors of
\infty
-categories
\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{C}
, with
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{D}
both allowed to vary. If, in the very next breath, we fix the target
\mathcal{C}
, then it makes more sense to use some sort of overcategory
(\mathbbm{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty})_{/\mathcal{C}}
from the beginning. There is a method to our madness; in
Section
5
, we will introduce a monoidal version of this construction, and here we will need to allow the targets of our functors to vary.

3.1
The enhanced twisted arrow category

For a 2-category
\mathsf{C}
, the twisted arrow 1-category
\mathbf{Tw}(\mathsf{C})
has the following description.

•

The objects of
\mathbf{Tw}(\mathsf{C})
are the morphisms of
\mathsf{C}
:

f\colon c\to c^{\prime}.

•

For two objects
(f\colon c\to c^{\prime})
and
(g\colon d\to d^{\prime})
, the morphisms
f\to g
are given by diagrams

•

The composition of morphisms is given by concatenating the corresponding diagrams.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}

In
[
6
]
, a homotopy-coherent version of the twisted arrow category of an
\infty
-bicategory is defined. For any
\infty
-bicategory
\mathbbm{C}
, the twisted arrow
\infty
-category of
\mathbbm{C}
, denoted
\mathbf{Tw}(\mathbbm{C})
, is a quasicategory whose
n
-simplices are diagrams
\Delta^{n}\star(\Delta^{n})^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbbm{C}
, together with a scaling to ensure that the information encoded in an
n
-simplex is determined, up to contractible choice, by data
