\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}^{n\text{ squares}}

in
\mathbbm{C}
. Here, the top row of morphisms corresponds to the spine of the
n
-simplex
\Delta^{n}\subset\Delta^{n}\star(\Delta^{n})^{\mathrm{op}}
, and the bottom row of morphisms to the spine of
(\Delta^{n})^{\mathrm{op}}\subset\Delta^{n}\star(\Delta^{n})^{\mathrm{op}}
. To make this correspondence more clear, we will introduce the following notation.

Notation 3.1.1
.

For
i\in[n]\subset[2n+1]
, we will write
\overline{i}:=2n+1-i
. Thus,
\overline{0}=2n+1
,
\overline{1}=2n
, etc.

Thus, the
i
th column in the above diagram corresponds to the image of the morphism
i\to\overline{i}
in
\Delta^{n}\star(\Delta^{n})^{\mathrm{op}}
.

The scaling mentioned above is defined in the following way.

Definition 3.1.2
.

We define a cosimplicial object
\tilde{Q}\colon\Delta\to\mathcal{S}\mathrm{et}_{\Delta}^{\mathrm{sc}}
by sending

\tilde{Q}([n])=(\Delta^{n}\star(\Delta^{n})^{\mathrm{op}},\dagger),

where
\dagger
is the scaling consisting of all degenerate 2-simplices, together with all 2-simplices of the following kinds:

1.

All simplices
\Delta^{2}\to\Delta^{n}\star(\Delta^{n})^{\mathrm{op}}
factoring through
\Delta^{n}
.

2.

All simplices
\Delta^{2}\to\Delta^{n}\star(\Delta^{n})^{\mathrm{op}}
factoring through
(\Delta^{n})^{\mathrm{op}}
.

3.

All simplices
\Delta^{\{i,j,\overline{k}\}}\subseteq\Delta^{n}\star(\Delta^{n})^{\mathrm{op}}
,
i<j\leq k
.

4.

All simplices
\Delta^{\{k,\overline{j},\overline{i}\}}\subseteq\Delta^{n}\star(\Delta^{n})^{\mathrm{op}}
,
i<j\leq k
.

Note that
\Delta^{n}\star(\Delta^{n})^{\mathrm{op}}\cong\Delta^{2n+1}
. We will use this identification freely.

This extends to a nerve-realization adjunction

Q:\mathcal{S}\mathrm{et}_{\Delta}\longleftrightarrow\mathcal{S}\mathrm{et}_{\Delta}^{\mathrm{sc}}:\mathbf{Tw},

where the functor
Q
is the extension by colimits of the functor
\tilde{Q}
, and for any scaled simplicial set
X
, the simplicial set
\mathbf{Tw}(X)
has
n
-simplices

\mathbf{Tw}(\mathbbm{C})_{n}=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{S}\mathrm{et}_{\Delta}^{\mathrm{sc}}}(Q(\Delta^{n}),X).

Notation 3.1.3
.

For any simplicial set
X
,
Q(X)
carries the scaling given simplex-wise by that described above. We will denote this also by
\dagger
. Using the identification
\Delta^{n}\star(\Delta^{n})^{\mathrm{op}}\cong\Delta^{2n+1}
, we can write
Q(\Delta^{n})
explicitly and compactly as
\Delta^{2n+1}_{\dagger}
, which we will often do.

The inclusion
\Delta^{n}\amalg(\Delta^{n})^{\mathrm{op}}\hookrightarrow\Delta^{n}\star(\Delta^{n})^{\mathrm{op}}
provides, for any
\infty
-bicategory
\mathbbm{C}
with underlying
\infty
-category
\mathcal{C}
, a morphism of simplicial sets

\mathbf{Tw}(\mathbbm{C})\to\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{op}}.

In
[
6
]
, the following is shown.

Theorem 3.1.4
.

Let
\mathbbm{C}
be an
\infty
-bicategory (presented as a fibrant scaled simplicial set), and let
\mathcal{C}
be the underlying
\infty
-category (presented as a quasicategory). Then the map

p_{\mathbbm{C}}\colon\mathbf{Tw}(\mathbbm{C})\to\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{op}}.

is a cartesian fibration between quasicategories, and a morphism
f\colon\Delta^{1}\to\mathbf{Tw}(\mathbbm{C})
is
p_{\mathbbm{C}}
-cartesian if and only if the morphism
\sigma\colon\Delta^{3}_{\dagger}\to\mathbbm{C}
to which it is adjunct is fully scaled, i.e. factors through the map
\Delta^{3}_{\dagger}\to\Delta^{3}_{\sharp}
. Furthermore, the cartesian fibration
p_{\mathbbm{C}}
classifies the functor

\mathrm{Map}_{\mathbbm{C}}(-,-)\colon\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}.

3.2
Two lemmas about marked-scaled anodyne morphisms

In the section following this one, we provide a fairly explicit construction of a family of marked-scaled anodyne inclusions. Constructing these inclusions directly from the classes of generating marked-scaled anodyne inclusions given in
Definition
1.5.1
would be possible but tedious. To lighten this load somewhat, we reproduce in this section two lemmas from
[
1
]
. The majority of this section is a retelling of
[
1
, Sec. 2.3 and 2.4]
. Note however that our needs will be rather different than those of
[
1
]
, and this is reflected in some minor differences of notation.

We first need a compact notation for specifying simplicial subsets of
\Delta^{n}
. Denote by
P\colon\mathcal{S}\mathrm{et}\to\mathcal{S}\mathrm{et}
the power set functor.

Definition 3.2.1
.

Let
T
be a finite linearly ordered set, so
T\cong[n]
for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
. For any subset
\mathcal{A}\subseteq P(T)
, define a simplicial subset

\mathcal{S}^{\mathcal{A}}_{T}:=\bigcup_{S\in\mathcal{A}}\Delta^{T\smallsetminus S}\subseteq\Delta^{T}.

Notation 3.2.2
.

If the linearly ordered set
T
is clear from context, we will drop it, writing
\mathcal{S}^{\mathcal{A}}
.

Note 3.2.3
.

The assignment
\mathcal{A}\mapsto\mathcal{S}^{\mathcal{A}}
does not induce is not a one-to-one correspondence between subsets
\mathcal{A}\subseteq P([n])
and simplicial subsets
S^{\mathcal{A}}\subseteq\Delta^{n}
, since for any two subsets
S\subsetneq S^{\prime}\subset[n]
, we have that
\Delta^{[n]\smallsetminus S^{\prime}}\subsetneq\Delta^{[n]\smallsetminus S}
.

Example 3.2.4
.

Take
T=[2]
. Taking
\mathcal{A}=\{\{2\},\{1,2\}\}
gives the simplicial subset
\mathcal{S}^{\mathcal{A}}_{T}=\Delta^{\{0,1\}}\cup\Delta^{\{0\}}=\Delta^{\{0,1\}}\subset\Delta^{2}
, as does taking
\mathcal{A}=\{\{2\}\}
.

It will be useful to consider two subsets
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\subseteq P(T)
to be equivalent if they produce the same simplicial subset of
\Delta^{T}
.

Definition 3.2.5
.

We will write
\mathcal{A}\sim\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
if
\mathcal{S}^{\mathcal{A}}=\mathcal{S}^{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}
.

The relation
\sim
is an equivalence relation, but we will not use this.

The set
P([n])
forms a poset, ordered by inclusion, and any
\mathcal{A}\subseteq P([n])
a subposet. An element
q
of a poset
Q
is said to be
minimal
if there are no elements of
Q
which are strictly less than
q
. By
Note
3.2.3
only the minimal elements of
\mathcal{A}
contribute to the union defining
\mathcal{S}^{\mathcal{A}}
. We have just shown the following.

Lemma 3.2.6
.

For any
\mathcal{A}\subseteq P([n])
, we have
\mathcal{A}\sim\mathrm{min}(\mathcal{A})
, where
\mathrm{min}(\mathcal{A})\subseteq\mathcal{A}
is the set of minimal elements of
\mathcal{A}
.

Simplicial subsets of the form
\mathcal{S}^{\mathcal{A}}_{T}
enjoy the following easily-proved calculation rules.

Lemma 3.2.7
.

Suppose
\mathcal{A}\subseteq P([n])
is a subset such that for all
S
,
T\in\mathcal{A}
, it holds that
S\cap T=\emptyset
. Then
\mathcal{S}^{\mathcal{A}}
contains each
k
-simplex of
\Delta^{n}
for all
k<{|\mathcal{A}|}-1
.

Proof.

Let
X\subseteq[n]
. The simplex
\Delta^{X}\to\Delta^{n}
factors through
\mathcal{S}^{\mathcal{A}}
if and only if it factors through
\Delta^{[n]\smallsetminus T}
for some (possibly not unique)
T\in\mathcal{A}
. This in turn is true if and only if
X
and
T
do not have any elements in common. For
{|X|}<{|\mathcal{A}|}
, there is always a set
T\in\mathcal{A}
which does not have any elements in common with
X
.
∎

Lemma 3.2.8
.

Let
\mathcal{A}\subseteq P([n])
and
T\subseteq[n]
. Then

\mathcal{S}^{\mathcal{A}}_{[n]}\cup\Delta^{T}=\mathcal{S}^{\mathcal{A}\cup\{[n]\smallsetminus T\}}_{[n]}.

The next lemma will be particularly useful in building inclusions
\mathcal{S}^{\mathcal{A}}_{[n]}\hookrightarrow\Delta^{n}
simplex-by-simplex.

Lemma 3.2.9
.

Let
\mathcal{A}\subset P([n])
, and let
T\subset[n]
. Then the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{S}^{\mathcal{A}|T}_{T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta^{T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{S}^{\mathcal{A}}_{[n]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{S}^{\mathcal{A}}_{[n]}\cup\Delta^{T}}

is bicartesian, where
\mathcal{A}|T
is the subset of
P(T)
given by

\mathcal{A}|T=\{S\cap T\mid S\in\mathcal{A}\}.

Proof.

We have an equality

\mathcal{S}_{T}^{\mathcal{A}|T}=\mathcal{S}^{\mathcal{A}}_{[n]}\cap\Delta^{T}.

as subsets of
\Delta^{n}
.
∎

Notation 3.2.10
.

For any marked-scaled
n
-simplex
(\Delta^{n},E,T)
we will by minor abuse of notation reuse the same letters
E
and
T
to denote the restriction of the markings and scalings to any simplicial subset
S\subseteq\Delta^{n}
.

In the remainder of this section we reproduce two lemmas from
[
1
]
which provide criteria for the inclusion
(\mathcal{S}^{\mathcal{A}},E,T)\subseteq(\Delta^{n},E,T)
to be marked-scaled anodyne.

Definition 3.2.11
.

Let
\mathcal{A}\subseteq P([n])
. We call
X\in P([n])
an
\mathcal{A}
-basal set
if it contains precisely one element from each
S\in\mathcal{A}
. We denote the set of all
\mathcal{A}
-basal sets by
\mathrm{Bas}(\mathcal{A})
.

Definition 3.2.12

(
[
6
]
, Definition 1.3)
.

We will call a subset
\mathcal{A}\subseteq P([n])

inner dull
if it satisfies the following conditions.

•

It does not include the empty set;
\emptyset\notin\mathcal{A}
.

•

There exists
0<i<n
such that for all
S\in\mathcal{A}
, we have
i\notin S
.

•

For every
S
,
T\in\mathcal{A}
, it follows that
S\cap T=\emptyset
.

•

For each
\mathcal{A}
-basal set
X\in P([n])
, there exist
u
,
v\in X
such that
u<i<v
.

The element
i\in[n]
is known as the
pivot point.

Note 3.2.13
.

The last condition of
Definition
3.2.12
is always satisfied if
\mathcal{A}
contains two singletons
\{u\}
and
\{v\}
such that
u<i<v
.

Definition 3.2.14
.

Let
\mathcal{A}\subseteq P([n])
be an inner dull subset with pivot point
i
, and let
X\in\mathcal{A}
. We will denote the adjacent elements of
X
surrounding
i
by
\ell^{X}<i<u^{X}
.

Lemma 3.2.15

(The Pivot Trick:
[
1
]
, Lemma 2.3.5)
.

Let
\mathcal{A}\subseteq P([n])
be an inner dull subset with pivot point
i
, and let
(\Delta^{n},E,T)
be a marked-scaled simplex. Further suppose that the following conditions hold:

1.

Every marked edge
e\in E
which does not contain
i
factors through
\mathcal{S}^{\mathcal{A}}
.

2.

For all scaled simplices
\sigma=\{a<b<c\}
of
\Delta^{n}
which do not factor through
\mathcal{S}^{\mathcal{A}}
, and which do not contain the pivot point
i
, we have
a<i<c
, and
\sigma\cup\{i\}
is fully scaled.

3.

For all
X\in\mathrm{Bas}(\mathcal{A})
and all
r
,
s\in[n]
such that
\ell^{X}\leq r<i<s\leq u^{X}
, the triangle
\{r,i,s\}
is scaled

Then the inclusion
(\mathcal{S}^{\mathcal{A}},E,T)\hookrightarrow(\Delta^{n},E,T)
is marked-scaled anodyne.

Definition 3.2.16
.

We will call a subset
\mathcal{A}\subseteq P([n])

right dull
if it satisfies the following conditions.

•

It is nonempty;
\mathcal{A}\neq\emptyset
.

•

It does not include the empty set;
\emptyset\notin\mathcal{A}
.

•

For all
S\in\mathcal{A}
, we have
n\notin S
.

•

For every
S
,
T\in\mathcal{A}
, it follows that
S\cap T=\emptyset
.

In this case, we will refer to
n
as the pivot point.

Lemma 3.2.17

(Right-anodyne pivot trick)
.

Let
\mathcal{A}\subset P([n])
be a right dull subset (whose pivot point is by definition
n
), and let
(\Delta^{n},E,T)
be a marked-scaled simplex. Further suppose that the following conditions hold:

•

Every marked edge
e\in E
which does not contain
n
factors through
\mathcal{S}^{\mathcal{A}}
.

•

Let
\sigma=\{a<b<c\}
be a scaled simplex not containing
n
. Then either
\sigma
factors through
\mathcal{S}^{\mathcal{A}}
, or
\sigma\cup\{n\}
is fully scaled, and
c\to n
is marked.

•

For all Z in
\mathrm{Bas}(\mathcal{A})
, For all
r
,
s\in[n]
with
r\leq\min(Z)\leq\max(Z)\leq s<n
, the triangle
\Delta^{\{r,s,n\}}
is scaled, and the simplex
\Delta^{\{s,n\}}
is marked.

Then the inclusion
(\mathcal{S}^{\mathcal{A}},E,T)\hookrightarrow(\Delta^{n},E,T)
is marked-scaled anodyne.

3.3
The infinity-category of local systems

In
Subsection
3.1
, we defined the twisted arrow category
\mathbf{Tw}(\mathbbm{C})
of an
\infty
-bicategory
\mathbbm{C}
, and noted that the natural cartesian fibration
\mathbf{Tw}(\mathbbm{C})\to\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{op}}
classifies the mapping functor
\mathrm{Map}_{\mathbbm{C}}(-,-)
. Thus, the objects
\mathbf{Tw}(\mathbbm{C})
are simply the morphisms in
\mathbbm{C}
.

We can use this to define, for any cocomplete
\infty
-category
\mathcal{C}
, the
\infty
-category of
\mathcal{C}
-local systems; we simply consider
\mathbf{Tw}(\mathbbm{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty})
, whose objects are all functors between
\infty
-categories, and restrict to those functors whose domain is an
\infty
-groupoid, and whose codomain is
\mathcal{C}
.

Definition 3.3.1
.

For any cocomplete
\infty
-category
\mathcal{C}
, we define the
\infty
-category of
\mathcal{C}
-local systems
\mathbf{LS}(\mathcal{C})
together with a map of simplicial sets
p\colon\mathbf{LS}(\mathcal{C})\to\mathcal{S}
by the following pullback diagram.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{LS}(\mathcal{C})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Tw}(\mathbbm{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{S}\times\{\mathcal{C}\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}\times\mathcal{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}^{\mathrm{op}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{\prime}}

(15)

Note 3.3.2
.

It is natural to wonder if we are making life unnecessarily difficult by not simply considering the category
\mathcal{S}\times_{\mathbbm{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}}(\mathbbm{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty})_{/\mathcal{C}}
, which is after all also an
\infty
-category of functors from spaces into
\mathcal{C}
. The reason for the more complicated construction given here will become apparent in
Subsection
5.3
, when we define the monoidal structure on
\mathbf{LS}(\mathcal{C})
.

It is shown in
[
6
]
that
p^{\prime}
, hence also
p
, is a cartesian fibration. The cartesian fibration
p
classifies the functor
\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathcal{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}
sending

f\colon X\to Y\qquad\longmapsto\qquad f^{*}\colon\mathrm{Fun}(Y,\mathcal{C})\to\mathrm{Fun}(X,\mathcal{C}).

Under the assumption that
\mathcal{C}
is cocomplete, each pullback map
f^{*}
has a left adjoint
f_{!}
, given by left Kan extension. It follows on abstract grounds that
p
is also a cocartesian fibration, whose cocartesian edges represent left Kan extension. In
Subsection
3.4
, we show this explicitly. Our goal in this section is to do some legwork to facilitate the proof of this result.

In investigating the map
p\colon\mathbf{LS}(\mathcal{C})\to\mathcal{S}
, it will be useful to factor the pullback square in
Equation
15
into the two pullback squares

\hbox to239.13pt{\vbox to52.64pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 119.56253pt\lower-26.31947pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

where
[\mathcal{C}]
denotes the path component of
\mathcal{C}
in
(\mathcal{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}^{\mathrm{op}})^{\simeq}
. In order to show that
p
is a cocartesian fibration, it will help us to understand the map
p^{\prime\prime}
. The
n
-simplices of
\mathcal{R}
are given by maps

Q(\Delta^{n})=(\Delta^{n}\star(\Delta^{n})^{\mathrm{op}})_{\dagger}\to\mathbbm{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}

such that

•

each object in
(\Delta^{n})^{\mathrm{op}}\subseteq Q(\Delta^{n})
is sent to a quasicategory which is equivalent to
\mathcal{C}
(and in particular cocomplete), and

•

each morphism in
(\Delta^{n})^{\mathrm{op}}\subseteq Q(\Delta^{n})
is mapped to an equivalence in
\mathbbm{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}
.

We can more usefully encode the second condition by endowing
\mathbbm{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}
and
Q
with a marking.

Definition 3.3.3
.

We define the following markings.

•

We denote by
\mathbbm{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}^{\natural}
the marked-scaled simplicial set whose underlying scaled simplicial set is
\mathbbm{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}
, and where all equivalences have been marked.

•

We denote by
\heartsuit
the marking on
\Delta^{n}\star(\Delta^{n})^{\mathrm{op}}
consisting of all morphisms belonging to
(\Delta^{n})^{\mathrm{op}}
, and by
(\Delta^{n}\star(\Delta^{n})^{\mathrm{op}})^{\heartsuit}_{\dagger}
the corresponding marked-scaled simplicial set.

Definition 3.3.4
.

We define a cosimplicial object

\tilde{R}\colon\Delta\to\mathcal{S}\mathrm{et}_{\Delta}^{\mathbf{MS}};\qquad[n]\mapsto(\Delta^{n}\star(\Delta^{n})^{\mathrm{op}})^{\heartsuit}_{\dagger}.

We denote the extension of
\tilde{R}
by colimits by

R\colon\mathcal{S}\mathrm{et}_{\Delta}\to\mathcal{S}\mathrm{et}_{\Delta}^{\mathbf{MS}};\qquad X\mapsto\colim_{\Delta^{n}\to X}R(n).

The marked-scaled simplicial sets
R(\Delta^{n})
are rather complicated, and showing that
p^{\prime\prime}
is a cartesian fibration by solving the necessary lifting problems explicitly would be impractical. We will instead replace
R(\Delta^{n})
by something simpler, considering the simplicial subsets coming from the inclusions

\Delta^{n}\star\Delta^{\{\overline{0}\}}\subseteq\Delta^{n}\star(\Delta^{n})^{\mathrm{op}},

(16)

which we understand to inherit the marking and scaling.

Definition 3.3.5
.

We will denote by
\tilde{J}\colon\Delta\to\mathcal{S}\mathrm{et}_{\Delta}^{\mathbf{MS}}
the cosimplicial object

\tilde{J}\colon\Delta\to\mathcal{S}\mathrm{et}_{\Delta}^{\mathbf{MS}};\qquad[n]\mapsto(\Delta^{n}\star\Delta^{\{\overline{0}\}})^{\heartsuit}_{\dagger},

and by
J
the extension by colimits

J\colon\mathcal{S}\mathrm{et}_{\Delta}\to\mathcal{S}\mathrm{et}_{\Delta}^{\mathbf{MS}};\qquad X\mapsto\colim_{\Delta^{n}\to X}\tilde{J}(n).

The inclusions of
Equation
16
induce for each
n\geq 0
an inclusion of marked-scaled simplicial sets

v_{n}\colon\tilde{J}(n)\to\tilde{R}(n).

Lemma 3.3.6
.

For all
n\geq 0
, the map
v_{n}
is marked-scaled anodyne, hence a weak equivalence in the model structure on marked-scaled simplicial sets.

We postpone the proof of
Lemma
3.3.6
until the end of this section.

Note 3.3.7
.

Some care is warranted: the
v_{n}
are not the components of a natural transformation
\tilde{J}\Rightarrow\tilde{R}
! The necessary squares simply do not commute for morphisms
\phi\colon[m]\to[n]
in
\Delta
such that
\phi(0)\neq 0
. This means that we do not, for a general simplicial set
X
, get a canonical weak equivalence of marked-scaled simplicial sets
J(X)\to R(X)
.

Despite the warning given in
Note
3.3.7
, it is still possible to get maps
J(X)\to R(X)
in some cases. In the remainder of this section, we check that we can produce a marked-scaled weak equivalence
J(\Lambda^{n}_{0})\to R(\Lambda^{n}_{0})
.

Notation 3.3.8
.

Denote by
\mathring{\Delta}
the subcategory of
\Delta
on morphisms
[m]\to[n]
which send
0\mapsto 0
. Denote by
I
the inclusion
\mathring{\Delta}\hookrightarrow\Delta
.

It is easy to check the following.

Lemma 3.3.9
.

The morphisms
v_{n}
form a natural transformation
v\colon\tilde{J}\circ I\Rightarrow\tilde{R}\circ I
.

Lemma 3.3.10
.

For all
n\geq 1
, there is a marked-scaled equivalence
J(\Lambda^{n}_{0})\to R(\Lambda^{n}_{0})
.

Proof.

We can write
J(\Lambda^{n}_{0})
as a colimit of the composition
\tilde{J}\circ b
coming from the bottom of the diagram

where
(\Delta\downarrow\Lambda^{n}_{0})^{\mathrm{nd}}
is the category of nondegenerate simplices of
\Lambda^{n}_{0}
. Denote by
P_{n}
the poset of proper subsets of
[n]
such that
0\in S
. There is an obvious inclusion
P_{n}\hookrightarrow(\Delta\downarrow\Lambda^{n}_{0})^{\mathrm{nd}}
, and one readily checks using Quillen’s Theorem A that this inclusion is cofinal. Further note that the functor
P_{n}\to(\Delta\downarrow\Lambda^{n}_{0})\to\Delta
factors through
\mathring{\Delta}
via a map
a\colon P_{n}\to\mathring{\Delta}
. Thus, we can equally express
J(\Lambda^{n}_{0})
as the colimit of the functor
\tilde{J}\circ I\circ a
. Precisely the same reasoning tells us that we can express
R(\Lambda^{n}_{0})
as the colimit of
\tilde{R}\circ I\circ a
. The result now follows from
Lemma
3.3.9
,
Lemma
3.3.6
, and the fact that each strict colimit is the model for the homotopy colimit.
∎

Proof of Lemma
3.3.6
.

In this proof, all simplicial subsets of
\Delta^{2n+1}
will be assumed to carry the marking
\heartsuit
and the scaling
\dagger
.

We can write each
v_{n}
as a composition

\Delta^{\{0,\ldots,n,\overline{0}\}}\overset{v^{\prime\prime}_{n}}{\hookrightarrow}\Delta^{\{0,\ldots,n,\overline{0}\}}\cup\Delta^{\{\overline{n},\ldots,\overline{0}\}}\overset{v^{\prime}_{n}}{\hookrightarrow}\Delta^{2n+1}.

Here, the map
v_{n}^{\prime\prime}
is a pushout along the inclusion
(\Delta^{\{n\}})^{\sharp}_{\sharp}\hookrightarrow(\Delta^{n})^{\sharp}_{\sharp}
, which is marked-scaled anodyne by
Proposition
1.5.2
. It remains to show that each of the maps
v_{n}^{\prime}
is marked-scaled anodyne.

To this end, we introduce some notation. Let
M^{n}_{0}=\Delta^{\{0,\ldots,n,\overline{0}\}}\cup\Delta^{\{\overline{n},\ldots\overline{0}\}}\subset\Delta^{2n+1}
, and for
1\leq k\leq n
, define

M^{n}_{k}:=M^{n}_{0}\cup\left(\bigcup_{\ell=1}^{k}\Delta^{[2n+1]\smallsetminus\{\ell,\overline{\ell}\}}\right).

There is an obvious filtration

M^{n}_{0}\overset{i^{n}_{0}}{\hookrightarrow}M^{n}_{1}\overset{i^{n}_{1}}{\hookrightarrow}\cdots\overset{i^{n}_{n-1}}{\hookrightarrow}M^{n}_{n}\overset{i^{n}_{n}}{\hookrightarrow}\Delta^{2n+1}.

(17)

Define

j^{n}_{k}:=i^{n}_{n}\circ\cdots\circ i^{n}_{k}\colon M^{n}_{k}\hookrightarrow\Delta^{2n+1},\qquad 0\leq k\leq n.

In particular, note that
j^{n}_{0}=v_{n}^{\prime}
.

This allows us to replace our goal by something superficially more difficult: we would like to show that, for each
n\geq 0
and each
0\leq k\leq n
, the map
i^{n}_{k}
is marked-scaled anodyne. We proceed by induction. We take as our base case
n=0
, where we have the trivial filtration

M^{0}_{0}\overset{i^{0}_{0}}{=}\Delta^{1}.

This is an equality of subsets of
\Delta^{1}
, hence certainly an equivalence.

We now suppose that the result holds true for
n-1
; that is, that the maps
i^{n-1}_{k}
are marked-scaled anodyne for all
0\leq k\leq n-1
. We aim to show that each
i^{n}_{k}
is marked-scaled anodyne for each
0\leq k\leq n
.

For
0\leq k<n
, we can write
i^{n}_{k}
as the inclusion

\mathcal{S}^{\mathcal{A}}_{[2n+1]}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{S}^{\mathcal{A}}_{[2n+1]}\cup\Delta^{[2n+1]\smallsetminus\{k,\overline{k}\}},\qquad\mathcal{A}=\left\{\begin{subarray}{c}\{\overline{n},\ldots,\overline{1}\}\\
\{0,\ldots n\}\\
\{1,\overline{1}\}\\
\vdots\\
\{k-1,\overline{k-1}\}\end{subarray}\right\}.

By
Lemma
3.2.9
, we have a pushout square

Therefore, it suffices to show that the top morphism is marked-scaled anodyne. One checks that this map is of the form
j^{n-1}_{k}
, and is thus marked-scaled anodyne by the inductive hypothesis. Therefore, it remains only to show that
i^{n}_{n}
is marked-scaled anodyne.

The case
n=0
is an isomorphism, so there is nothing to show. We treat the case
n=1
separately. In this case,
i^{1}_{1}
takes the form

\Delta^{\{0,1,\overline{0}\}}\cup\Delta^{\{\overline{1},\overline{0}\}}\overset{v_{1}^{\prime}}{\hookrightarrow}\Delta^{3},

which we construct in the following way.

1.

First we fill the simplex
\Delta^{\{1,\overline{1},\overline{0}\}}
(together with its marking and scaling) as a pushout along a morphism of type
(A2)
.

2.

Then we fill the simplex
\Delta^{\{0,1,\overline{1}\}}
(together with its marking and scaling) as a pushout along a morphism of type
(A1)
.

3.

Finally, we fill the full simplex
\Delta^{3}
(together with its marking and scaling) as a pushout along a morphism of type
(A1)
.

Now we may assume that
n\geq 2
. In this case, we can write
i^{n}_{n}\colon M^{n}_{n}\hookrightarrow\Delta^{2n+1}
as an inclusion

\mathcal{S}^{\mathcal{A}}_{[2n+1]}\subseteq\Delta^{2n+1},\qquad\mathcal{A}=\left\{\begin{subarray}{c}\{\overline{n},\ldots,\overline{1}\}\\
\{0,\ldots n\}\\
\{1,\overline{1}\}\\
\vdots\\
\{n,\overline{n}\}\end{subarray}\right\}.

We will express this inclusion as the following composition of fillings:

1.

We first add the simplices
\Delta^{\{n,\overline{n},\ldots,\overline{0}\}}
,
\Delta^{\{n-1,n,\overline{n},\ldots,\overline{0}\}}
, …,
\Delta^{\{2,\ldots,n,\overline{n},\ldots,\overline{0}\}}
.

2.

We next add
\Delta^{\{0,\ldots,n,\overline{n},\overline{0}\}}
,
\Delta^{\{0,\ldots,n,\overline{n},\overline{n-1},\overline{0}\}}
, …,
\Delta^{\{0,\ldots,n,\overline{n},\ldots,\overline{2},\overline{0}\}}

3.

We next add
\Delta^{\{1,\ldots n,\overline{n},\ldots,\overline{0}\}}
.

4.

We finally add
\Delta^{\{0,\ldots n,\overline{n},\ldots,\overline{0}\}}
.

We proceed.

1.

•

Using
Lemma
3.2.9
, we see that the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{S}^{\mathcal{A}|\{n,\overline{n},\ldots,\overline{0}\}}_{\{n,\overline{n},\ldots,\overline{0}\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta^{\{n,\overline{n},\ldots,\overline{0}\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{S}^{\mathcal{A}}_{[2n+1]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{S}^{\mathcal{A}}_{[2n+1]}\cup\Delta^{\{n,\overline{n},\ldots,\overline{0}\}}}

is pushout. In order to show that the bottom inclusion is marked-scaled anodyne, it thus suffices to show that the top inclusion is marked-scaled anodyne. We have

\hbox to174.17pt{\vbox to77.32pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 87.08469pt\lower-38.65973pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-87.08469pt}{-2.5pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}}}{{}}}}

This is a dull subset of
\{n,\overline{n},\ldots,\overline{0}\}
with pivot
\overline{n}
. The only
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
-basal set is
\{n,\overline{n-1},\ldots,\overline{1}\}
. One checks that
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
, together with the marking
\heartsuit
and scaling
\dagger
, satisfies the conditions of the
Pivot Trick
:

\circ

For
n=2
, the only the marked edge not containing the pivot
\overline{2}
is
\overline{1}\to\overline{0}
, which belongs to
\mathcal{S}^{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}_{\{n,\overline{n},\ldots,\overline{0}\}}
. The only scaled simplex which does not contain
\overline{2}
, and which does not factor through
\mathcal{S}^{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}
, is
\sigma=\{2<\overline{1}<\overline{0}\}
. The simplex
\sigma\cup\{\overline{2}\}
is fully scaled.

\circ

For
n=3
, each marked edge is contained in
\mathcal{S}^{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}
. The only scaled triangle which does not contain the pivot
\overline{3}
, and which does not factor through
\mathcal{S}^{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}
, is
\sigma=\{3<\overline{2}<\overline{1}\}
. The simplex
\sigma\cup\{\overline{3}\}
is fully scaled.

\circ

For
n\geq 4
, all scaled and marked simplices belong to
\mathcal{S}^{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}
by
Lemma
3.2.7
.

In each case, the simplex
\{n,\overline{n},\overline{n-1}\}
is scaled, so the top inclusion is marked-scaled anodyne by the
Pivot Trick
. We can write

\mathcal{S}^{\mathcal{A}}_{[2n+1]}\cup\Delta^{\{n,\overline{n},\ldots,\overline{0}\}}=\mathcal{S}^{\mathcal{A}\cup([2n+1]\smallsetminus\{n,\overline{n},\ldots,\overline{0}\})}_{[2n+1]},

We see that

\mathcal{A}\cup([2n+1]\smallsetminus\{n,\overline{n},\ldots,\overline{0}\})\sim\left\{\begin{subarray}{c}\{\overline{n},\ldots,\overline{1}\}\\
\{0,\ldots n-1\}\\
\{1,\overline{1}\}\\
\vdots\\
\{n,\overline{n}\}\end{subarray}\right\},

which we denote by
\mathcal{A}_{n-1}
.

•

We proceed inductively. Suppose we have added the simplices
\Delta^{\{n,\overline{n},\ldots,\overline{0}\}}
,
\Delta^{\{n-1,n,\overline{n},\ldots,\overline{0}\}}
, …,
\Delta^{\{k+1,\ldots,n,\overline{n},\ldots,\overline{0}\}}
, for
2\leq k\leq n-1
. Using
Lemma
3.2.8
and
Lemma
3.2.6
can write the result of these additions as

\mathcal{S}^{\mathcal{A}}_{[2n+1]}\cup\left(\bigcup_{i=k+1}^{n}\Delta^{\{i,\ldots,n,\overline{n},\ldots,\overline{0}\}}\right)=\mathcal{S}^{\mathcal{A}_{k+1}}_{[2n+1]},

where

\mathcal{A}_{k+1}=\left\{\begin{subarray}{c}\{\overline{n},\ldots,\overline{1}\}\\
\{0,\ldots,k\}\\
\{1,\overline{1}\}\\
\vdots\\
\{n,\overline{n}\}\end{subarray}\right\}.

Using
Lemma
3.2.9
, we see that the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{S}^{\mathcal{A}_{k+1}|\{k,\ldots,n,\overline{n},\ldots,\overline{0}\}}_{\{k,\ldots,n,\overline{n},\ldots,\overline{0}\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta^{\{k,\ldots,n,\overline{n},\ldots,\overline{0}\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{S}^{\mathcal{A}_{k+1}}_{[2n+1]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{S}^{\mathcal{A}_{k+1}}_{[2n+1]}\cup\Delta^{\{k,\ldots,n,\overline{n},\ldots,\overline{0}\}}}

is pushout, so in order to show that the bottom morphism is marked-scaled anodyne, it suffices to show that the top morphism is. We see that

\mathcal{A}_{k+1}|\{k,\ldots,n,\overline{n},\ldots,\overline{0}\}=\left\{\begin{subarray}{c}\{\overline{n},\ldots,\overline{1}\}\\
\{k\}\\
\{\overline{1}\}\\
\vdots\\
\{\overline{k-1}\}\\
\{k,\overline{k}\}\\
\vdots\\
\{n,\overline{n}\}\end{subarray}\right\}\sim\left\{\begin{subarray}{c}\{k\}\\
\{\overline{1}\}\\
\vdots\\
\{\overline{k-1}\}\\
\{k+1,\overline{k+1}\}\\
\vdots\\
\{n,\overline{n}\}\end{subarray}\right\}=:\mathcal{A}_{k+1}^{\prime}.

This is a dull subset of
P(\{k,\ldots,n,\overline{n},\ldots,\overline{0}\})
with pivot
\overline{k}
, and one checks that the conditions of the
Pivot Trick
are satisfied:

\circ

For
n=3
, where the only value of
k
is
k=2
, each marked edge is contained in
\mathcal{S}^{\mathcal{A}_{k+1}^{\prime}}
by
Lemma
3.2.7
, and one can check that each scaled triangle factors through
\mathcal{S}^{\mathcal{A}_{k+1}^{\prime}}
.

\circ

For
n\geq 4
, all scaled and marked simplices belong to
\mathcal{S}^{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}
by
Lemma
3.2.7
.

The basal sets are of the form

\{k,a_{1},\ldots,a_{n-k},\overline{k-1},\ldots,\overline{1}\},

where each
a_{1}
, …,
a_{n-k}
is of the form
\ell
or
\overline{\ell}
for
k+1\leq\ell\leq n
. In each case, the simplex
\{a_{n-k},\overline{k},\overline{k-1}\}
is scaled. Thus, the conditions of the
Pivot Trick
, the top morphism is marked-scaled anodyne.

We have now added the simplices promised in part 1., and are left with the simplicial subset
\mathcal{S}^{\mathcal{A}_{2}}_{[2n+1]}
, where

\mathcal{A}_{2}=\left\{\begin{subarray}{c}\{\overline{n},\ldots,\overline{1}\}\\
\{0,1\}\\
\{1,\overline{1}\}\\
\vdots\\
\{n,\overline{n}\}\end{subarray}\right\}.

2.

Each step in this sequence is solved exactly like those above. The calculations are omitted. The end result is the simplicial subset

\mathcal{B}_{2}=\left\{\begin{subarray}{c}\{\overline{1}\}\\
\{0,1\}\\
\{2,\overline{2}\}\\
\vdots\\
\{n,\overline{n}\}\end{subarray}\right\}.

3.

Using
Lemma
3.2.9
, we see that the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{S}^{\mathcal{B}_{2}|\{1,\ldots,n,\overline{n},\ldots,\overline{0}\}}_{\{1,\ldots,n,\overline{n},\ldots,\overline{0}\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta^{\{1,\ldots,n,\overline{n},\ldots,\overline{0}\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{S}^{\mathcal{B}_{2}}_{[2n+1]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{S}^{\mathcal{B}_{2}}_{[2n+1]}\cup\Delta^{\{1,\ldots,n,\overline{n},\ldots,\overline{0}\}}}

is pushout. We thus have to show that the top morphism is marked-scaled anodyne. We have

\mathcal{B}_{2}|\{1,\ldots,n,\overline{n},\ldots,\overline{0}\}\sim\left\{\begin{subarray}{c}\{\overline{1}\}\\
\{2,\overline{2}\}\\
\vdots\\
\{n,\overline{n}\}\\
\{\overline{1}\}\end{subarray}\right\}.

One readily sees that this is a right-dull subset, and that the conditions of the
Right-Anodyne Pivot Trick
are satisfied.

4.

One solves this as before, checking that the conditions of the
Pivot Trick
are satisfied, with pivot point
2
.

∎

3.4
The map governing local systems is a cocartesian fibration

in which both squares are pullback, is a cocartesian fibration. We now define the class of morphisms in
\mathbf{LS}(\mathcal{C})
which we claim are
p
-cocartesian.

Definition 3.4.1
.

A morphism
\tilde{\sigma}\colon\Delta^{1}\to\mathbf{LS}(\mathcal{C})
is said to be
left Kan
if the simplex
\sigma\colon\Delta^{3}_{\dagger}\to\mathbbm{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}
to which it is adjoint has the property that the restriction
\sigma|\Delta^{\{0,1,\overline{0}\}}
is left Kan in the sense of
Definition
2.0.1
.

We draw the ‘front’ and ‘back’ of a general
3
-simplex
\sigma\colon\Delta^{3}_{\dagger}\to\mathbbm{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}
corresponding to some morphism
\Delta^{1}\to\mathbf{LS}(\mathcal{C})
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\zeta}

and

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{C}}}

Here, the
2
-simplices which are notated without natural transformations are constrained by the scaling
\dagger
to be thin. The morphism
\tilde{\sigma}
is left Kan if and only if
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
is a left Kan extension of
\mathcal{F}
along
f
, and
\eta
is a unit map.

Notation 3.4.2
.

We endow the quasicategory
\mathbf{LS}(\mathcal{C})
with the marking
\clubsuit\subseteq\mathbf{LS}(\mathcal{C})_{1}
consisting of all left Kan edges.

Theorem 3.4.3
.

The map
p\colon\mathbf{LS}(\mathcal{C})\to\mathcal{S}
is a cocartesian fibration, and an edge
\Delta^{1}\to\mathbf{LS}(\mathcal{C})
is
p
-cocartesian if and only if it is left Kan.

Proof.

By
[
10
, Prop. 3.1.1.6]
, it suffices to show that the map
\mathbf{LS}(\mathcal{C})^{\clubsuit}\to\mathcal{S}^{\sharp}
has the right lifting property with respect to each of the classes of generating marked anodyne morphisms. We check these one-by-one.

1.

We need to check that all lifting problems

\hbox to121.57pt{\vbox to55.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 60.78412pt\lower-27.69838pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-60.78412pt}{-21.53865pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{
{}{}{}}{}{
{}{}{}}

We showed in
Lemma
3.3.6
that the lower morphism in this square is a marked-scaled equivalence. We would now like to show that the upper morphism is a marked-scaled equivalence. Recall that, since the cofibrations in the model structure on marked-scaled simplicial sets are simply those morphisms whose underlying morphism of simplicial sets is a monomorphism, the colimits defining
b
are models for the homotopy colimits, so the result follows from the observation that each component is a weak equivalence in the marked-scaled model structure.

Using

, Prop. A.2.3.1]

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J(\Lambda^{n}_{0})\displaystyle\coprod_{(\Lambda^{n}_{0})^{\flat}_{\sharp}\amalg(\Delta^{\{\overline{0}\}})^{\sharp}_{\sharp}}(\Delta^{n})^{\flat}_{\sharp}\amalg(\Delta^{\{\overline{0}\}})^{\sharp}_{\sharp}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbbm{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell}

has a solution whose restriction

is left Kan. However, we note that there exists a pushout square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\Lambda^{\{0,\ldots,n,\overline{0}\}}_{0})^{\flat}_{\flat}}

3.

We need to show that every lifting problem of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\Lambda^{2}_{1})^{\sharp}\amalg_{(\Lambda^{2}_{1})^{\flat}}(\Delta^{2})^{\sharp}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{LS}(\mathcal{C})^{\clubsuit}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\Delta^{2})^{\sharp}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{S}^{\sharp}}

has a solution. Considering the adjoint lifting problem, we find that it suffices to show that for any
\sigma\colon\Delta^{5}_{\dagger}\to\mathbbm{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}
such that the restrictions
\sigma|\{0,1,\overline{0}\}
and
\sigma|\{1,2,\overline{1}\}
are left Kan and the morphisms belonging to
\sigma|\{\overline{2},\overline{1},\overline{0}\}
are equivalences, the restriction
\sigma|\{0,2,\overline{0}\}
is left Kan. Applying
Lemma
2.5.1
to
\sigma|\{1,2,\overline{1},\overline{0}\}
, we see that
\sigma|\{1,2,\overline{1}\}
is left Kan if and only if
\sigma|\{1,2,\overline{0}\}
is left Kan. Applying
Lemma
2.5.3
to
\sigma|\{0,1,2,\overline{0}\}
guarantees that
\sigma|\{0,2,\overline{0}\}
is left Kan as required.

4.

We need to show that for all Kan complexes
K
, the lifting problem

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{\flat}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{LS}(\mathcal{C})^{\clubsuit}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{\sharp}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{S}^{\sharp}}

has a solution. To see this, note that each morphism in
K
must be mapped to an equivalence in
\mathbf{LS}(\mathcal{C})
, and a morphism
a\colon\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{G}
in
\mathbf{LS}(\mathcal{C})
is an equivalence and only if it is
p
-cartesian, and lies over an equivalence in
\mathcal{S}
.

•

By
Theorem
3.1.4
, the morphism
a
is
p
-cartesian if and only if the map
\sigma\colon\Delta^{3}_{\dagger}\to\mathbbm{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}
to which it is adjoint factors through the map
\Delta^{3}_{\dagger}\to\Delta^{3}_{\sharp}
. In particular, the restriction
\sigma|\Delta^{\{0,1,\overline{0}\}}
is thin.

•

The image of
a
in
\mathcal{S}
is the restriction
\sigma|\Delta^{\{0,1\}}
, which is therefore an equivalence.

It follows from
Example
2.1.8
that the morphism
a
is automatically left Kan. Thus, each morphism in
K
is mapped to a left Kan morphism in
\mathbf{LS}(\mathcal{C})
, and we are justified in marking them; our lifting problems admit solutions.

∎

4
Pull-push of local systems

For any cocomplete
\infty
-category
\mathcal{C}
, we have constructed an
\infty
-category
\mathbf{LS}(\mathcal{C})
of local systems on
\mathcal{C}
, together with a map
p\colon\mathbf{LS}(\mathcal{C})\to\mathcal{S}
. We have further shown that this map is a bicartesian fibration.

•

As a cartesian fibration, it classifies the pullback functor
(-)^{*}\colon\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathcal{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}
; for any morphism
X\to Y
in
\mathcal{S}
, this functoriality gives us a map

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}\colon\mathrm{Fun}(Y,\mathcal{C})\to\mathrm{Fun}(X,\mathcal{C}).}

•

As a cocartesian fibration, it classifies the left Kan extension functor
(-)_{!}\colon\mathcal{S}\to\mathcal{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}
; for any morphism
X\to Y
, in
\mathcal{S}
, this functoriality gives us a map

f_{!}\colon\mathrm{Fun}(X,\mathcal{C})\to\mathrm{Fun}(Y,\mathcal{C}).

We might wonder if we can combine these functorialities. Given a
span
of morphisms in
\mathcal{S}
, i.e. a diagram in
\mathcal{S}
of the form

Our goal in this section is to upgrade this construction to a functor
\hat{r}\colon\mathrm{Span}(\mathcal{S})\to\mathcal{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}
, where
\mathrm{Span}(\mathcal{S})
is an
\infty
-category with the following rough description.

•

The objects of
\mathrm{Span}(\mathcal{S})
are the same as the objects of
\mathcal{S}
, i.e. spaces
X
,
Y
, etc.

•

The morphisms from
X
to
X^{\prime}
are given by spans in
\mathcal{S}
, i.e. diagrams of the form given in
Equation
19
.

•

The
2
-simplices witnessing the composition of two morphisms

X\overset{h}{\leftarrow}Y\overset{f}{\rightarrow}X^{\prime}\quad\text{and}\quad X^{\prime}\overset{g}{\leftarrow}Y\overset{j}{\rightarrow}X^{\prime\prime}

are diagrams of the form

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}},

agree up to a specified natural equivalence. The arrows
h^{*}
and
j_{!}
are the same in both cases, and can be ignored. This condition can thus be distilled down to the so-called
Beck-Chevalley condition:

•

For any pullback square in
\mathcal{S}

the comparison map

f_{!}\circ g^{*}\overset{\eta}{\Rightarrow}f_{!}\circ g^{*}\circ(f^{\prime})^{*}\circ(f^{\prime})_{!}\overset{\simeq}{\Rightarrow}f_{!}\circ f^{*}\circ(g^{\prime})^{*}\circ(f^{\prime})_{!}\overset{\epsilon}{\Rightarrow}(g^{\prime})^{*}\circ(f^{\prime})_{!}

is an equivalence.

One can phrase the Beck-Chevalley condition at the level of fibrations rather than functors into
\mathcal{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}
. This is a classical definition, here more or less lifted from
[
7
]
.

Definition 4.0.1
.

A bicartesian fibration of quasicategories
p\colon\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{T}
such that
\mathcal{T}
admits pullbacks is called a
Beck-Chevalley fibration
if it has the following property.

(BC)

For any commuting square in
\mathcal{X}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

lying over a pullback square in
\mathcal{T}
, if the morphism
f
is
p
-cocartesian and the morphisms
g
and
g^{\prime}
are
p
-cartesian, then the morphism
f^{\prime}
is
p
-cocartesian.

In fact, it turns out that this condition is sufficient to guarantee that the the pull-push procedure is functorial.

Proposition 4.0.2
.

Let
p\colon\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{T}
be a Beck-Chevalley fibration. Then there is a functor
\mathrm{Span}(\mathcal{T})\to\mathcal{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}
sending an object
t\in\mathrm{Span}(\mathcal{T})
to the fiber
\mathcal{X}_{t}
, and a span
t\overset{b}{\leftarrow}s\overset{a}{\rightarrow}t^{\prime}
to the composition
a_{!}\circ b^{*}\colon\mathcal{X}_{t}\to\mathcal{X}_{t^{\prime}}
.

Proof.

It follows immediately from
Theorem
1.6.2
that there is a cocartesian fibration

\tilde{p}\colon\mathrm{Span}^{\mathrm{cart}}(\mathcal{X})\to\mathrm{Span}(\mathcal{T}),

where
\mathrm{Span}^{\mathrm{cart}}
denotes the category of spans whose backwards-facing legs are constrained to be
p
-cartesian; that is,
\mathcal{X}^{\dagger}=\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{cart}}
. Straightening gives the result that we want.
∎

We would like to show that pull-push of local systems is functorial. According to
Proposition
4.0.2
, it suffices to show the following, the proof of which will come at the end of this section.

Proposition 4.0.3
.

The functor
p\colon\mathbf{LS}(\mathcal{C})\to\mathcal{S}
is a Beck-Chevalley fibration.

We first prove a helpful lemma. This is simply a reformulation of the definition of a left Kan extension (
Definition
2.1.6
) in the special case that we consider left Kan extensions along maps of Kan complexes.

Lemma 4.0.4
.

Let
f\colon X\to Y
be a map between Kan complexes, let
F\colon X\to\mathcal{C}
and
G\colon Y\to\mathcal{C}
be functors, and let
\eta\colon F\to G\circ y
be a natural transformation. Then
\eta
exhibits
G
as a left Kan extension of
F
along
f
if and only if, for all
y\in Y
, the natural transformatin
F\circ\pi\Rightarrow\underline{G(y)}
defined by the pasting diagram

where the middle square is a strict pullback. Since the map
Y^{\Delta^{1}}\times_{Y}X\to Y
is a Kan fibration, the middle square is also a homotopy pullback. We note that the left and right squares are also homotopy pullbacks, since the horizontal morphisms are weak equivalences. Thus, the outer square is a homotopy pullback; one easily checks that the natural equivalence
f\circ\pi\Rightarrow\underline{y}
defined by pasting the three squares agrees with the natural transformation
\alpha
defined in
Notation
2.1.5
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
4.0.3
.

We have already shown that
p
is a bicartesian fibration, and we know that
\mathcal{S}
admits pullbacks. Therefore, it suffices to show that
p
has
Property (BC)
.

We consider a square
\sigma\colon\Delta^{1}\times\Delta^{1}\cong\Delta^{\{0,1,2\}}\amalg_{\Delta^{\{0,2\}}}\Delta^{\{0,1^{\prime},2\}}\to\mathbf{LS}(\mathcal{C})
with the following properties.

1.

The restriction
\sigma|\Delta^{\{0,1\}}
is
p
-cartesian.

2.

The restriction
\sigma|\Delta^{\{1^{\prime},2\}}
is
p
-cartesian.

3.

The restriction
\sigma|\Delta^{\{1,2\}}
is
p
-cocartesian.

4.

The square
\sigma
lies over a pullback square

in
\mathcal{S}
.

We need to show that
\sigma|\Delta^{\{0,1^{\prime}\}}
is
p
-cocartesian.

The map
\sigma
adjunct to a map

\tau\colon\Delta^{\{0,1,2,\overline{2},\overline{1},\overline{0}\}}_{\dagger}\amalg_{\Delta^{\{0,2,\overline{2},\overline{0}\}}_{\dagger}}\Delta^{\{0,1^{\prime},2,\overline{2},\overline{1}^{\prime},\overline{0}\}}_{\dagger}\to\mathbbm{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}

such that
\tau|\Delta^{\{\overline{2},\overline{1},\overline{0}\}}\amalg_{\Delta^{\{\overline{2},\overline{0}\}}}\Delta^{\{\overline{2},\overline{1}^{\prime},\overline{0}\}}
is the constant functor with value
\mathcal{C}
. We can read off the following further properties of
\tau
, corresponding to the properties of
\sigma
above.

1.

The simplices
\tau|\Delta^{\{0,1,\overline{0}\}}
and
\tau|\Delta^{\{0,\overline{1},\overline{0}\}}
are thin.

2.

The simplices
\tau|\Delta^{\{1^{\prime},2,\overline{1}^{\prime}\}}
and
\tau|\Delta^{\{1^{\prime},\overline{2},\overline{1}^{\prime}\}}
are thin.

3.

The simplex
\tau|\Delta^{\{1,2,\overline{1}\}}
is left Kan.

4.

The restriction
\tau|\Delta^{\{0,1,2\}}\amalg_{\Delta^{\{0,2\}}}\Delta^{\{0,1^{\prime},2\}}
is equal to
p(\sigma)
.

The condition that
\sigma|\Delta^{\{0,1^{\prime}\}}
is
p
-cocartesian corresponds to the condition that the simplex
\tau|\Delta^{\{0,1,\overline{0}\}}
is left Kan.

The diagram
\tau
contains a lot of redundant data, which we would now like to consolidate. We first shuffle some data around our diagram. Applying
Lemma
2.5.1
to the simplex
\tau|\Delta^{\{1,2,\overline{1},\overline{0}\}}
, we see that
\tau|\Delta^{\{1,2,\overline{0}\}}
is left Kan. Applying
Lemma
2.5.2
to the simplex
\tau|\Delta^{\{1^{\prime},2,\overline{1}^{\prime},\overline{0}\}}
, we see that
\tau|\Delta^{\{1^{\prime},2,\overline{0}\}}
is thin.

We can now study a subdiagram which contains all the information we need. We consider the restriction

\tau|\Delta^{\{0,1,2,\overline{0}\}}\amalg_{\Delta\{0,2,\overline{0}\}}\Delta^{\{0,1^{\prime},2,\overline{0^{\prime}}\}}.

This is determined, up to specific choices of compositions, by the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{16.92505pt}{5.29095pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

Here, the square pictured is
\sigma
, and the triangle is
\tau|\Delta^{\{1^{\prime},2,\overline{0}\}}
.

Since the simplices
\tau|\Delta^{\{0,1,\overline{0}\}}
and
\tau|\Delta^{\{1,2,\overline{0}\}}
are thin, the pasting of the square and the triangle above is homotopic to the restriction
\tau|\Delta^{\{0,1,\overline{0}\}}
. This is the triangle which we wish to show is left Kan. According to
Lemma
4.0.4
it suffices to show that for all
x\in X_{1}
, the pasting diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X_{0})_{/x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{1^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{x\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}

exhibits
G(f(x))
as the colimit of
F\circ f^{\prime}\circ\pi
. But applying the pasting law for homotopy pullbacks, this follows directly from the assumption that
\eta
exhibits
G
as a left Kan extension of
F
along
f
.
∎

5
Monoidal pull-push of local systems

The classical Grothendieck construction provides an equivalence between functors
\hat{p}\colon\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{C}\mathrm{at}
and cartesian fibrations
p\colon\int\hat{p}\to\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{op}}
. There are many conditions one can impose upon the functor
\hat{p}
, and many structures one can endow it with; it is natural to wonder whether these properties and structures can be captured in the fibration
p
.

An answer is known in the case that
\hat{p}
is lax monoidal (the author learned about this fact from
[
12
]
). Suppose
p\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
is a cartesian fibration of 1-categories which classifies a pseudofunctor
\hat{p}\colon\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathcal{C}\mathrm{at}
. Further suppose the following:

•

The category
\mathcal{C}
carries a symmetric monoidal structure
(\otimes,I_{\mathcal{C}},\ldots)
.

•

The category
\mathcal{D}
carries a symmetric monoidal structure
(\boxtimes,I_{\mathcal{D}},\ldots)
.

•

The functor
p
is strong monoidal: for objects
x
,
y\in\mathcal{C}
, we have that
p(x\otimes y)=p(x)\boxtimes p(y)
, and
p(I_{\mathcal{C}})=I_{\mathcal{D}}
.
4
4

4

The reason for the strict equality in this condition is that we have chosen specific monoidal functors
\otimes
and
\boxtimes
, and specific unit objects
I_{\mathcal{C}}
and
I_{\mathcal{D}}
. Later, we will replace these strict choices by fibrations which give the same data up to coherent homotopy.

•

The monoidal product
\otimes
preserves
p
-cartesian morphisms in the sense that if
f
and
g
are
p
-cartesian morphisms, then
f\otimes g
is also
p
-cartesian.

Under these conditions, the pseudofunctor
\hat{p}
carries a lax monoidal structure
(\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{op}},\boxtimes)\to(\mathcal{C}\mathrm{at},\times)
, described as follows.

•

Restricting the tensor product

\otimes\colon\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}

to the fibers
\mathcal{C}_{d}
and
\mathcal{C}_{d^{\prime}}
of
p
over
d
and
d^{\prime}
yields the structure maps

\hat{p}(d)\times\hat{p}(d^{\prime})\to\hat{p}(d\boxtimes d^{\prime}).

That these structure maps form a pseudonatural transformation, i.e. that the necessary squares commute up to specified homotopy, follows from the assumption that the monoidal product preserves
p
-cartesian morphisms in each slot.

•

The requirement that
p(I_{\mathcal{C}})=I_{\mathcal{D}}
can be rephrased to say that
I_{\mathcal{D}}\in\hat{p}(I_{\mathcal{C}})
. This gives us the structure map
\ast\to\hat{p}(I_{\mathcal{D}})
, where
\ast
is the unit object in
\mathcal{C}\mathrm{at}
.

If we want to study covariant rather than contravariant pseudofunctors, we should replace cartesian fibrations by cocartesian fibrations; of course, now we must assume that
\otimes
preserves cocartesian edges rather than cartesian. The rest of the theory remains unchanged. These results also remain true in the
\infty
-categorical case, as we will show in
Subsection
5.1
.

As we saw in the last chapter, if we have a bicartesian fibration which satisfies the Beck-Chevalley condition (i.e. a Beck-Chevalley fibration), we can combine both cartesian and cocartesian functorialities into pull-push functoriality. Our aim in
Subsection
5.2
will be to show that the theory of Beck-Chevalley fibrations admits a monoidal generalization. More specifically, we will show the following. Consider a Beck-Chevalley fibration
r\colon\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{T}
such that
\mathcal{X}
carries a symmetric monoidal structure
\otimes
, and
\mathcal{T}
carries a symmetric monoidal structure
\boxtimes
. Under the assumption that
r
is strong monoidal, and that
\otimes
preserves both cartesian and cocartesian edges, the induced functor

\mathrm{Span}(\mathcal{T})\to\mathcal{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}

constructed in the previous chapter carries a lax monoidal structure. Note that this statement is not entirely original. A similar result in a somewhat different context is proved in
[
3
]
.

We will then apply our results to local systems. We will show that if
\mathcal{C}
is a symmetric monoidal
\infty
-category, then the
\infty
-category of
\mathcal{C}
-local systems carries a symmetric monoidal structure, defined on objects by

(\mathcal{F}\colon X\to\mathcal{C})\otimes(\mathcal{G}\colon Y\to\mathcal{C})\quad=\quad X\times Y\overset{\mathcal{F}\times\mathcal{G}}{\to}\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{C}\overset{\otimes}{\to}\mathcal{C}

and that the functor
\mathbf{LS}(\mathcal{C})\to\mathcal{S}
is can be given the structure of a monoidal Beck-Chevalley fibration, thus classifying a lax monoidal functor

(\mathrm{Span}(\mathcal{S}),\tilde{\times})\to(\mathcal{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty},\times).

5.1
The lax monoidal Grothendieck construction

Our aim in this section is to show an
\infty
-categorical version of the statement that a monoidal cartesian fibration
p\colon(\mathcal{C},\otimes)\to(\mathcal{D},\boxtimes)
such that
\otimes
preserves
p
-cartesian morphisms straightens to a lax monoidal pseudofunctor
\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathcal{C}\mathrm{at}
(as explained more concretely in the introduction to
Section
5
). We will assume familiarity with the theory of symmetric monoidal
\infty
-categories as laid out in
[
8
, Chap. 2]
; roughly, a symmetric monoidal
\infty
-category is defined to be a cocartesian fibration classifying a commutative monoid in
\mathcal{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}
. Our first goal will be to rephrase some of the results there in terms of cartesian fibrations rather than cocartesian fibrations.

Notation 5.1.1
.

We will frequently refer to the following maps in
\mathcal{F}\mathrm{in}_{*}^{\mathrm{op}}
.

•

Denote by
\rho^{i}\colon\langle n\rangle\to\langle 1\rangle
the map in
\mathcal{F}\mathrm{in}_{*}
sending
i\mapsto 1
and everything else to
\ast
. Denote the same map in
\mathcal{F}\mathrm{in}_{*}^{\mathrm{op}}
by
\rho_{i}
.

•

Denote by
\mu\colon\langle 2\rangle\to\langle 1\rangle
the active map in
\mathcal{F}\mathrm{in}_{*}
. We will denote the same map in
\mathcal{F}\mathrm{in}_{*}^{\mathrm{op}}
also by
\mu
.

Definition 5.1.2
.

A
CSMC
(contravariantly-presented symmetric monoidal
\infty
-category) is a cartesian fibration
p\colon\mathcal{C}_{\otimes}\to\mathcal{F}\mathrm{in}_{*}^{\mathrm{op}}
such that the contravariant transport maps
\rho_{i}^{*}\colon(\mathcal{C}_{\otimes})_{\langle n\rangle}\to(\mathcal{C}_{\otimes})_{\langle 1\rangle}
are the canonical projections exhibiting
(\mathcal{C}_{\otimes})_{\langle n\rangle}
as an
n
-fold homotopy product.

Notation 5.1.3
.

For any CSMC
\mathcal{C}_{\otimes}\to\mathcal{F}\mathrm{in}_{*}^{\mathrm{op}}
, we will denote the fiber
(\mathcal{C}_{\otimes})_{\langle 1\rangle}
simply by
\mathcal{C}
.

The equivalence
(\mathcal{C}_{\otimes})_{\langle n\rangle}\simeq\mathcal{C}^{n}
allow us to trade maps into
(\mathcal{C}_{\otimes})_{\langle n\rangle}
for
n
maps into
\mathcal{C}
, well-defined up to equivalence. Given a diagram
a\colon K\to(\mathcal{C}_{\otimes})_{\langle n\rangle}
, we will often abuse this terminology by calling any such corresponding diagrams
a_{i}\colon K\to\mathcal{C}
‘the’ components of
a
, as long as we are making reference only to properties of these components which are preserved under equivalence.

Definition 5.1.4
.

A
map
between CSMCs
q\colon\mathcal{C}_{\otimes}\to\mathcal{F}\mathrm{in}_{*}^{\mathrm{op}}
and
p\colon\mathcal{D}_{\boxtimes}\to\mathcal{F}\mathrm{in}_{*}^{\mathrm{op}}
is a functor
r
making the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}_{\otimes}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}_{\boxtimes}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}\mathrm{in}_{*}^{\mathrm{op}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}

commute. Given a map of CSMCs as above, we will further make use of the following terminology.

•

The map
r
is a
monoidal functor
if it sends
q
-cartesian morphisms to
p
-cartesian morphisms.

This implies, for example, that
r(x\otimes y)\simeq r(x)\boxtimes r(y)
, and that
r(I_{\mathcal{C}})\simeq I_{\mathcal{D}}
. It also automatically implies that the diagrams encoding associativity, etc., commute up to coherent homotopy.

•

An edge
f
in
(\mathcal{C}_{\otimes})_{\langle n\rangle}
is
componentwise cartesian (resp. componentwise cocartesian)
if for each
1\leq i\leq n
, the transport
\rho_{i}^{*}(f)
is
r|\langle 1\rangle
-cartesian (resp. cocartesian).

We can think of a morphism
f
in
(\mathcal{C}_{\otimes})_{\langle n\rangle}
as an
n
-tuple of morphisms
f_{i}
in
\mathcal{C}
. We say that
f
is componentwise (co)cartesian if each component
f_{i}
is (co)cartesian as an edge of the underlying fibration
\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
.

•

The tensor product
\otimes

preserves cartesian (resp. cocartesian) edges
if for all
\phi\colon\langle n\rangle\leftarrow\langle m\rangle\in\mathcal{F}\mathrm{in}_{*}^{\mathrm{op}}
, the associated functor
\phi^{*}\colon(\mathcal{C}_{\otimes})_{\langle m\rangle}\to(\mathcal{C}_{\otimes})_{\langle n\rangle}
sends componentwise cartesian (resp. componentwise cocartesian) morphisms contained in the fiber
(\mathcal{C})_{\langle m\rangle}
to componentwise cartesian (resp. componentwise cocartesian) morphisms contained in the fiber
(\mathcal{C}_{\otimes})_{\langle n\rangle}
.

We will show in
Lemma
5.1.5
that this is equivalent to demanding that if morphisms
f
and
g
in
\mathcal{C}
are
r|\langle 1\rangle
-(co)cartesian, then
f\otimes g
is as well.

•

The CSMC
q\colon\mathcal{C}_{\otimes}\to\mathcal{F}\mathrm{in}_{*}^{\mathrm{op}}
is
cartesian-compatible (resp. cocartesian-compatible)
an edge
f
in the fiber
(\mathcal{C}_{\otimes})_{\langle n\rangle}
is
r
-cartesian (resp. cocartesian) if and only if it is componentwise cartesian.

This is a technical condition which is true for all CSMCs which we will consider; it says that the way we encode the monoidal structure on
\mathcal{C}
into a cartesian fibration is well-behaved with respect to the fibration
r
.

Lemma 5.1.5
.

Let

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}_{\otimes}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}_{\boxtimes}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}\mathrm{in}_{*}^{\mathrm{op}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}

be a map between CSMCs. The tensor product
\otimes
preserves cartesian (resp. cocartesian) morphisms if and only if for all
r|\langle 1\rangle
-cartesian (resp.
r|\langle 1\rangle
-cocartesian) morphisms
f
and
g
in
\mathcal{C}
, the morphism
f\otimes g
is also
r|\langle 1\rangle
-cartesian (resp.
r|\langle 1\rangle
-cocartesian).

Proof.

We consider the cartesian case. The cocartesian case is identical.

Suppose that
\otimes
preserves
r
-cartesian morphisms, and let
f
and
g
be
r|\langle 1\rangle
-cartesian morphisms in
\mathcal{C}
. Then there is a morphism
[f,g]
in
(\mathcal{C}_{\otimes})_{\langle 2\rangle}
with components
f
and
g
. Taking
\phi
to be the active map
\langle 2\rangle\to\langle 1\rangle
shows that
f\otimes g
is
r|\langle 1\rangle
-cartesian.

Conversely, suppose that for any
r|\langle 1\rangle
-cartesian morphisms
f
and
g
, the morphism
f\otimes g=\phi^{*}([f,g])
in
\mathcal{C}
is
r|\langle 1\rangle
-cartesian. By associativity of the tensor product and induction, we have that the
n
-ary product of
r|\langle 1\rangle
-cartesian morphisms is again
r|\langle 1\rangle
-cartesian for
n\geq 2
. The image of the map
\alpha^{*}
, for
\alpha\colon\langle 0\rangle\to\langle 1\rangle
, i.e. the 0-ary tensor product, is equivalent to
\mathrm{id}_{I}
, where
I
is the unit object of
\mathcal{C}
, and is thus an equivalence, hence also
r|\langle 1\rangle
-cartesian.

Now let
\psi\colon\langle m\rangle\to\langle n\rangle
be a general map in
\mathcal{F}\mathrm{in}_{*}
, and let
f=[f_{1},\ldots,f_{m}]
be a componentwise cartesian map. The
i
th component of
\psi^{*}(f)
is given by
\bigotimes_{\psi(j)=i}f_{j}
, which is
r|\langle 1\rangle
-cartesian no matter the cardinality of
\psi^{-1}(i)
. Thus,
\psi^{*}(f)
is
r
-cartesian.
∎

Lemma 5.1.6
.

Let
q\colon\mathcal{C}_{\otimes}\to\mathcal{F}\mathrm{in}^{\mathrm{op}}
and
p\colon\mathcal{D}_{\otimes}\to\mathcal{F}\mathrm{in}_{*}^{\mathrm{op}}
be CSMCs, and let
r
be an inner fibration
\mathcal{C}_{\otimes}\to\mathcal{D}_{\boxtimes}
such that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}_{\otimes}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}_{\boxtimes}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}\mathrm{in}_{*}^{\mathrm{op}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}

commutes. Then the following are equivalent.

1.

The map
r
is a cartesian fibration.

2.

The map
r
has the following properties.

(a)

The restriction
r|\langle 1\rangle
is a cartesian fibration.

(b)

The map
r
is a monoidal functor.

(c)

The tensor product
\otimes
preserves cartesian edges.

(d)

The CSMC
q
is cartesian-compatible.

Proof.

Suppose that 1. holds, i.e. that
r
is a cartesian fibration. Then a) holds:
r|\langle 1\rangle
is a cartesian fibration because the pullback of a cartesian fibration is again a cartesian fibration.

We now show that b) holds, i.e. that the map
r
sends
q
-cartesian morphisms to
p
-cartesian morphisms. To this end, let
f\colon c\to c^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}_{\otimes}
be a
q
-cartesian morphism, and consider the image
p(f)\colon r(c)\to r(c^{\prime})
in
\mathcal{D}_{\otimes}
. Let
g\colon\tilde{d}\to r(c^{\prime})
be a
p
-cartesian lift of
q(f)\in\mathcal{F}\mathrm{in}_{*}^{\mathrm{op}}
, and
\hat{g}\colon\hat{d}\to c^{\prime}
a
r
-cartesian lift of
g
. By
[
10
, Prop. 2.4.1.3]
,
\hat{g}
is a
q
-cartesian lift of
q(f)
, so
f
and
\hat{g}
are equivalent as morphisms in
\mathcal{C}_{\otimes}
. Thus
r(f)
and
g
are equivalent as morphisms in
\mathcal{D}_{\otimes}
, so
r(f)
is
p
-cartesian since
g
is. This proves b).

We now show that d) holds. We do this in two steps. First, we show that if some morphism
f
in
(\mathcal{C}_{\otimes})_{\langle n\rangle}
is
r
-cartesian, then for all
\phi\colon\langle n\rangle\to\langle m\rangle
, the morphism
\phi^{*}(f)
is
r
-cartesian. This follows from the fact that for a square in
\mathcal{C}_{\otimes}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{0}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{1}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi^{*}(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

in which
u
and
v
are
q
-cartesian (and hence
r
-cartesian) and
f
is
r
-cartesian,
\phi^{*}(f)
is also
r
-cartesian. Applying this result to the inert maps
\rho^{i}\colon\langle n\rangle\to\langle 1\rangle
implies that if a morphism
f
in
(\mathcal{C}_{\otimes})_{\langle n\rangle}
is
r
-cartesian, then the components
f_{i}
are
r
-cartesian, hence also
r|\langle 1\rangle
-cartesian. This is one direction of d).

We now show the other direction of d): if a morphism in
(\mathcal{C}_{\otimes})_{\langle n\rangle}
is componentwise cartesian, then it is
r
-cartesian. To see this, let
f_{i}
be
r|\langle 1\rangle
-cartesian morphisms,
1\leq i\leq n
. We can find a morphism
f\in(\mathcal{C}_{\otimes})_{\langle n\rangle}
with components
f_{i}
. We would like to show that
f
is
r
-cartesian. To this end, consider the morphisms
r(f_{i})=\overline{f}_{i}
. We can find a morphism
\overline{f}
in
(\mathcal{D}_{\boxtimes})_{\langle n\rangle}
with components
\overline{f}_{i}
, and can take a
r
-cartesian lift
\hat{f}
of
\overline{f}
. The components of
\hat{f}
are equivalent to the
f_{i}
, and are hence
r
-cartesian. But then
f
is equivalent to
\hat{f}
, so
f
is
r
-cartesian as promised. This proves d).

We have now shown that under the assumption that 1. holds, a the pushforward maps preserve
r
-cartesian morphisms. This, together with d), proves c).

Now, suppose that 2. holds. We immediately note that c) implies that for each
\langle n\rangle\in\mathcal{F}\mathrm{in}_{*}^{\mathrm{op}}
, the restriction
r|\langle n\rangle
is a cartesian fibration, and an edge is
r|\langle n\rangle
-cartesian if and only if it is
r
-cartesian.

We now show that
r
admits cartesian lifts. Let
f\colon d\to d^{\prime}
be an edge in
\mathcal{D}_{\boxtimes}
lying over an edge
\phi\colon\langle n\rangle\leftarrow\langle m\rangle
in
\mathcal{F}\mathrm{in}_{*}^{\mathrm{op}}
, and let
c^{\prime}
be a lift of
d^{\prime}
to
\mathcal{C}_{\otimes}
. We can take a
q
-cartesian lift
g\colon c^{\prime\prime}\to c
of
f
, whose image in
\mathcal{D}_{\boxtimes}
is by b) a
p
-cartesian map
h\colon d^{\prime\prime}\to d^{\prime}
. This gives us the solid data

\hbox to124.73pt{\vbox to50.88pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 62.36624pt\lower-27.04419pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}

Using the fact that
h
is
p
-cartesian, we can fill the
2
-simplex in
\mathcal{D}_{\boxtimes}
, giving us in particular a map
j\colon d\to d^{\prime\prime}
. We can lift
j
to an
r|\langle n\rangle
-cartesian morphism
k\colon c\to c^{\prime\prime}
, which is therefore also
r
-cartesian. Using that
r
is an inner fibration, we can compose
g
and
k
relative to the simplex in
\mathcal{D}_{\boxtimes}
, giving us a lift
\ell
of
f
. But
\ell
is the composition of two
r
-cartesian morphisms, and hence itself
r
-cartesian.
∎

Note 5.1.7
.

Lemma
5.1.6
gives us an
\infty
-categorical version of the monoidal Grothendieck construction of
[
12
]
discussed at the begininning of this section. There, a monoidal structure is modelled as tuple
(\mathcal{C},\otimes,\dots)
. Because of the specific choice of a bifunctor
\otimes
and the surrounding corresponding coherence data, strong conditions must be placed on the functor
r
: it must be monoidal ‘on the nose.’ Here, we model our monoidal structure as a cartesian fibration, leaving these choices unmade. The strong notion of monoidality is thus replaced by the usual one.

The connection between the above result and the monoidal Grothendieck construction, somewhat explicitly, is as follows.
Lemma
5.1.6
tells us that the data of a functor
(\mathcal{C},\otimes)\to(\mathcal{D},\boxtimes)
satisfying conditions analogous to those given in
[
12
]
is equivalent to a cartesian fibration between the corresponding CSMCs. A cartesian fibration
r
between CSMCs
p\colon\mathcal{C}_{\otimes}\to\mathcal{F}\mathrm{in}_{*}^{\mathrm{op}}
and
q\colon\mathcal{D}_{\boxtimes}\to\mathcal{F}\mathrm{in}_{*}^{\mathrm{op}}
as above straightens to a
\mathcal{D}_{\boxtimes}^{\mathrm{op}}
-monoid in
\mathcal{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}
. But a
\mathcal{D}_{\boxtimes}^{\mathrm{op}}
-monoid in
\mathcal{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}
can be essentially uniquely extended to an
\mathcal{D}_{\boxtimes}^{\mathrm{op}}
-algebra object in
\mathcal{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty,\times}

[
8
, Prop 2.4.2.4–2.4.2.6]
, which is to say, a lax monoidal functor
(\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{op}},\boxtimes)\to(\mathcal{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty},\times)
.

In the remainder of this section, we study a special type of CSMC, those whose tensor product is given by the cartesian product.

Definition 5.1.8
.

A
CSMC

p\colon\mathcal{D}_{\boxtimes}\to\mathcal{F}\mathrm{in}_{*}^{\mathrm{op}}
is
cartesian
if the unit object
\ast
is final, and if for all objects
X
and
Y
in
\mathcal{D}
, the canonical maps
X\times\ast\leftarrow X\boxtimes Y\to\ast\times Y
exhibit
X\boxtimes Y
as the product of
X
and
Y
.

Example 5.1.9
.

Let
\mathcal{T}
be a category with finite products. Then
\mathcal{T}^{\mathrm{op}}
admits finite coproducts, and we can consider the cocartesian monoidal structure

(\mathcal{T}^{\mathrm{op}})^{\amalg}\to\mathcal{F}\mathrm{in}_{*}

of
[
8
, Construction 2.4.3.1]
. Taking the opposite of this functor gives us a cartesian fibration

\mathcal{T}_{\times}\to\mathcal{F}\mathrm{in}_{*}^{\mathrm{op}}.

That this is a cartesian CSMC follows immediately from the fact that
(\mathcal{T}^{\mathrm{op}})^{\amalg}\to\mathcal{F}\mathrm{in}_{*}
is a cocartesian symmetric monoidal category.

In any category
\mathcal{T}
with pullbacks, one can form a category
\mathrm{Span}(\mathcal{T})
of spans in
\mathcal{T}
. If
\mathcal{T}
also has a terminal object, hence all finite limits (including, of course, finite products), then the category of spans inherits a monoidal structure via

We now construct the monoidal structure on this category of spans explicitly, starting from any cartesian CSMC
\mathcal{T}_{\times}\to\mathcal{F}\mathrm{in}_{*}^{\mathrm{op}}
.

Proposition 5.1.10
.

Suppose
\mathcal{T}_{\times}\to\mathcal{F}\mathrm{in}_{*}^{\mathrm{op}}
is a cartesian CSMC whose underlying category
\mathcal{T}
admits pullbacks. Then there exists a (cocartesian-presented) symmetric monoidal category
\mathrm{Span}(\mathcal{T})^{\times}\to\mathcal{F}\mathrm{in}_{*}
.

Proof.

We upgrade this functor to a functor of triples. We will consider the following triples.

•

We define a triple structure
(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{F}_{\dagger},\mathcal{F}^{\dagger})
on
\mathcal{F}\mathrm{in}_{*}^{\mathrm{op}}
, where

\circ

\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{F}\mathrm{in}_{*}^{\mathrm{op}}

\circ

\mathcal{F}_{\dagger}=(\mathcal{F}\mathrm{in}_{*}^{\mathrm{op}})^{\simeq}

\circ

\mathcal{F}^{\dagger}=\mathcal{F}\mathrm{in}_{*}^{\mathrm{op}}

This is obviously adequate.

•

We define a triple
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{T}_{\dagger},\mathcal{T}^{\dagger})
as follows.

\circ

\mathcal{T}=\mathcal{T}_{\times}

\circ

\mathcal{T}_{\dagger}=\mathcal{T}_{\times}\times_{\mathcal{F}\mathrm{in}_{*}^{\mathrm{op}}}(\mathcal{F}\mathrm{in}_{*}^{\mathrm{op}})^{\simeq}

\circ

\mathcal{T}^{\dagger}=\mathcal{T}_{\times}

To see that this is adequate, we first note that
p
admits relative pullbacks. To see this, note that each fiber admits pullbacks by virtue our assumption that
\mathcal{T}
admits pullbacks, and the identifications
(\mathcal{T}_{\times})_{\langle n\rangle}\simeq\mathcal{T}^{n}
. The functoriality coming from
p
implements products, and therefore commute with pullbacks. Thus,
\mathcal{T}_{\times}
admits pullbacks, and
p
preserves pullbacks. This immediately implies that
(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{T}_{\dagger},\mathcal{T}^{\dagger})
is adequate:

1.

Pullbacks of this form are simply squares with horizontal morphisms given by equivalences.

2.

Any pullback square lies over a pullback square in
\mathcal{F}\mathrm{in}_{*}^{\mathrm{op}}
, so this reduces to the lemma about cartesian morphisms.

We thus consider the map of triples

\pi\colon(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{T}_{\dagger},\mathcal{T}^{\dagger})\to(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{F}_{\dagger},\mathcal{F}^{\dagger}).

One readily checks that
\pi
satisfies the conditions of
Theorem
1.6.1
:

•

The first condition holds because cocartesian morphisms lying over equivalences are themselves equivalences, hence also cartesian, so we can solve the relevant lifting problems using cartesian lifts.

•

The second condition holds because a square whose bottom-horizontal morphism is an equivalence is pullback if and only if its top-horizontal morphism is an equivalence.

This gives us a functor
\mathrm{Span}(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{T}_{\dagger},\mathcal{T}^{\dagger})\to\mathrm{Span}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{F}_{\dagger},\mathcal{F}^{\dagger})
. Pulling back along the equivalence
\mathcal{F}\mathrm{in}_{*}\to\mathrm{Span}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{F}_{\dagger},\mathcal{F}^{\dagger})
gives us the cocartesian fibration functor
\mathrm{Span}(\mathcal{T})^{\times}\to\mathcal{F}\mathrm{in}_{*}
. It remains only to check that the maps
\rho^{i}_{*}\colon\mathrm{Span}(\mathcal{T})^{\times}_{\langle n\rangle}\to\mathrm{Span}(\mathcal{T})^{\times}_{\langle 1\rangle}
are the canonical projections exhibiting

\mathrm{Span}(\mathcal{T})^{\times}_{\langle n\rangle}\simeq\left(\mathrm{Span}(\mathcal{T})^{\times}_{\langle 1\rangle}\right)^{n}.

In order to show this, we should show that for each simplicial set
K
, the map
a
in the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}(K,\mathrm{Span}(\mathcal{T})^{\times}_{\langle n\rangle})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathrm{Map}(K,\mathrm{Span}(\mathcal{T})^{\times}_{\langle 1\rangle})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}^{\mathrm{aCart}}(\mathrm{sd}(K),(\mathcal{T}_{\times})_{\langle n\rangle})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathrm{Map}^{\mathrm{aCart}}(\mathrm{sd}(K),(\mathcal{T}_{\times})_{\langle 1\rangle})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}(\mathrm{sd}(K),(\mathcal{T}_{\times})_{\langle n\rangle})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathrm{Map}(\mathrm{sd}(K),(\mathcal{T}_{\times})_{\langle 1\rangle})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

is an isomorphism in the category
\mathrm{h}\mathbf{Kan}
. In order to show this, it suffices to show that the map
b
is an isomorphism in
\mathrm{h}\mathbf{Kan}
. We note that the morphisms
i
and
j
are inclusions of connected components, and the map
c
is an isomorphism because
\mathcal{T}_{\times}\to\mathcal{F}\mathrm{in}_{*}^{\mathrm{op}}
is a CSMC, so in order to show that
b
is an isomorphism in
\mathrm{h}\mathbf{Kan}
, it suffices to show that it is essentially surjective. This follows from the fact that a square in
(\mathcal{T}_{\times})_{\langle n\rangle}\simeq((\mathcal{T}_{\times})_{\langle 1\rangle})^{n}
is pullback if and only if each component is pullback.
∎

5.2
Monoidal Beck-Chevalley fibrations

In this section, we combine several results from earlier sections.

•

In
Section
4
, we showed that a bicartesian fibration
p\colon\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{T}
satisfying the Beck-Chevalley condition allows us to combine the functoriality
\mathcal{T}\to\mathcal{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}
and
\mathcal{T}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathcal{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}
into push-pull functoriality
\mathrm{Span}(\mathcal{T})\to\mathcal{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}
. We called such bicartesian fibrations
Beck-Chevalley fibrations.

•

In
Subsection
5.1
we showed that a cartesian fibration
p\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
between monoidal categories whose tensor products were subject to certain compatibility conditions classifies a lax monoidal functor
\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathcal{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}
; and dually, that a cocartesian fibration
p
satisfying dual compatibility conditions classifies a lax monoidal functor
\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}
.

We will now show that a Beck-Chevalley fibration
p\colon\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{T}
between monoidal categories whose tensor products satisfy appropriate compatibility conditions classifies a lax monoidal functor
\mathrm{Span}(\mathcal{T})\to\mathcal{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}
. We will call such fibrations
monoidal Beck-Chevalley fibrations.

Definition 5.2.1
.

A
monoidal Beck-Chevalley fibration
is a functor
r
of CSMCs

lying over the cube in
\mathcal{F}\mathrm{in}_{*}^{\mathrm{op}}
, whose diagonal morphisms are
p
-cartesian, and whose front face is pullback by assumption. The bottom face is pullback since it lies over a pullback square in
\mathcal{F}\mathrm{in}_{*}^{\mathrm{op}}
and the diagonal morphsims are
p
-cartesian, and the top face is pullback because the diagonal morphisms are equivalences. The cube lemma thus implies that the back square is pullback. Note that the back square is entirely contained in the fiber over
\langle n\rangle
, and thus can be thought of as consisting of
n
component squares in
\mathcal{T}
; by the equivalence
(\mathcal{T}_{\otimes})_{\langle n\rangle}\simeq(\mathcal{T})^{n}
, these component squares are themselves pullback.

We now return our attention to the diagram in
\mathcal{X}_{\otimes}
. The morphism
f
is
r
-cocartesian by assumption. That the tensor product
\otimes
preserves
r
-cocartesian morphisms implies that
h
is
r
-cocartesian. Applying the Beck-Chevalley condition componentwise to the back face yields that
h^{\prime}
is componentwise cocartesian, hence
r
-cocartesian. Since
f^{\prime}
is equivalent to
h^{\prime}
,
f^{\prime}
is also
r
-cocartesian. Thus, d) holds.

We now show that 2. implies 1. Restricting each of the conditions of 2. to the fibers over
\langle 1\rangle
immediately implies that
r|\langle 1\rangle
is a Beck-Chevalley fibration, and b) implies that
r
is monoidal, and that
q
is cartesian-compatible. It remains to show that the tensor product
\otimes
preserves
r
-cartesian and
r
-cocartesian morphisms. To this end, consider a square
